Quantum Harish-Chandra isomorphism for the double affine Hecke algebra of GL n
Joshua Jeishing Wen

Abstract
. We prove that for generic parameters, the quantum radial parts map of Varagnolo and Vasserot gives an isomorphism between the spherical double affine Hecke algebra of G ​ L n GL_{n} and a quantized multiplicative quiver variety, as defined by Jordan.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 20C08, 33D52, 53D55; Secondary: 16G20.

1.
Introduction

This paper proves a quantum/multiplicative analogue of the
Harish-Chandra isomorphism
, a result at the source of many fruitful directions of research.
For a complex reductive group
G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{t}
, and Weyl group
W
, the classical isomorphism is concerned with the ring of differential operators
D(\mathfrak{g})
.
Harish-Chandra’s
radial parts
map
[
HC64
]
is a homomorphism
D(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\rightarrow D(\mathfrak{t})^{W}
that is in some sense a restriction to
\mathfrak{t}
“performed with extra steps”.
It was proven to be surjective by Wallach
[
Wal93
]
and Levasseur–Stafford
[
LS95
]
, and the latter also determined its kernel
[
LS96
]
: the adjoint action induces a homomorphism
\mu:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow D(\mathfrak{g})
and the kernel is the
G
invariants of the left ideal
\mathfrak{I}:=D(\mathfrak{g})\mu(\mathfrak{g})
.
Altogether, we have a description of
D(\mathfrak{t})^{W}
as a
quantum Hamiltonian reduction
:

\left[D(\mathfrak{g})\big/\mathfrak{I}\right]^{G}\cong D(\mathfrak{t})^{W}

The radial parts map is of fundamental importance in the application of rings of differential operators to geometric representation theory (see references cited above).

In the case
G=GL_{n}
, this construction admits a 1-parameter deformation, discovered by Etingof–Ginzburg
[
EG02
]
.
The smash product
D(\mathfrak{h})\rtimes W
can be deformed via a parameter
c
to the
rational Cherednik algebra

H_{n}(c)
of the symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
;
D(\mathfrak{h})^{W}
is then replaced with the
spherical subalgebra

SH_{n}(c)
.
On the other side, we consider
D(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{n})
.
The adjoint and vector representations give a map
\mu:\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\rightarrow D(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{n})
, and the deformation parameter
c
appears in the ideal via the trace character
\mathrm{tr}:\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{c}

\displaystyle:=D(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{n})\big((\mu-c\mathrm{tr})(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\big)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{c}

\displaystyle:=\left[D(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{n})\big/\mathfrak{I}_{c}\right]^{G}

The
deformed Harish-Chandra isomorphism
was proved by Gan–Ginzburg
[
GG06
]
:

(1.1)

\mathfrak{A}_{c}\cong SH_{n}(c)

\mathfrak{A}_{c}
is a natural quantization of the Hilbert scheme
n
points in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, constructed via Hamiltonian reduction as a
Nakajima quiver variety
for the Jordan quiver:

(1.2)

Various constructions exist
[
BFG06
,
GS06
,
KR08
]
to microlocalize modules for
\mathfrak{A}_{c}
into coherent sheaves on the Hilbert scheme, and the isomorphism (
1.1
) allows a rich interplay between such sheaves and representations of
H_{n}(c)
; in particular,
H_{n}(c)
itself microlocalizes to Haiman’s
Procesi bundle

[
Hai01
]
.

The ladder of deformation affords us more rungs
1
1

1

Let us that mention that the intermediate step relating differential operators on
GL_{n}
and the
trigonmetric
DAHA was done by Finkelberg–Ginzburg
[
FG10
]
.
—the rational Cherednik algebra is itself a degeneration of the double affine Hecke algebra (DAHA)
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
, an algebra that has appeared across many fields since its initial discovery and application by Cherednik to solving conjectures from the theory of Macdonald polynomials
[
Che95
]
.
It is natural to ask if there is an analogue of the Harish-Chandra isomorphism for
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
and, more specifically, its spherical subalgebra
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
.
One can view the ring
D(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
as
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{gl}_{n}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{gl}_{n}]
with a nontrivial commutation relation between the two tensorands.
It is almost immediately obvious that
D(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
would need to be replaced by an algebra quantizing
\mathbb{C}[GL_{n}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[GL_{n}]
; this is no longer the coordinate ring of a cotangent bundle and thus there is no quantization via differential operators that is available to us “out of the box”.
Moreover, to perform quantum Hamiltonian reduction, the algebra structure of the quantization needs to be equivariant with respect to whatever symmetry object replaces
GL_{n}
.

The correct quantization was defined by Varagnolo–Vasserot
[
VV10
]
: this is their ring of
quantum differential operators
on
GL_{n}
, which we denote by
\mathcal{D}
.
Here,
GL_{n}
-equivariance is replaced with equivariance with respect to the quantized universal enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}:=U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
.
As a
\mathcal{U}
-module,
\mathcal{D}
is isomorphic to the tensor product of two copies of
\mathcal{O}:=\mathcal{O}_{q}(GL_{n})
, an equivariant version of functions on quantum
GL_{n}
.
One can construct
\mathcal{O}
from the braided monoidal category of finite-dimensional
\mathcal{U}
-modules via a braided analogue of Tannakian reconstruction discovered by Majid
[
Maj93
]
, and it is also a localization of what is called the
reflection equation algebra

[
DM03
]
.

\mathcal{D}
also appeared in prior work of Alekseev–Schomerus
[
AS96
]
on quantizations of character varieties.

Varagnolo–Vasserot also address the other necessary ingredients, but we follow the definitions of Jordan in his construction of
quantized multiplicative quiver varieties

[
Jor14
]
.
Out of the same quiver data (
1.2
), this yields a
\mathbb{C}(q,t)
-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{t}
through a Hopf-algebraic version of quantum Hamiltonian reduction.
Our main result is the following:

Main Theorem
.

The quantized multiplicative quiver variety
\mathcal{A}_{t}
for the quiver data (
1.2
) is isomorphic as a
\mathbb{C}(q,t)
-algebra to the spherical
GL_{n}
-DAHA
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
.

Thus, the two algebras are isomorphic for generic values of the parameters
q
and
t
.
Prior to our result, the analogous isomorphism was proven in the following cases:

•

when
q=1

[
Obl04
]
;

•

when
q
is a root of unity of sufficiently large order
[
VV10
]
;

•

formally over the ring
\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
where
q=e^{\hbar}

[
Jor14
]
;

•

for any
q\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
n=2

[
BJ18
]
.

Our strategy follows the well-established pattern from the rational case
[
GG06
,
EGGO07
]
.
Both sides of the isomorphism are invariant subalgebras, and thus one does not have a presentation for either; from a general perspective, one may be curious about techniques for proving two algebras are isomorphic without generators and relations.
In the rational case, the scheme of proof goes as follows:

(1)

embed
SH_{n}(c)
into a ring of differential operators via a
Dunkl representation
;

(2)

map
\mathfrak{A}_{c}
to that same ring via a deformed analogue of the Harish-Chandra radial parts map;

(3)

show that the radial parts map is injective and surjects onto the image of
SH_{n}(c)
.

\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
has an analogue of (1), the
Dunkl-Cherednik
embedding into a ring of difference operators.
Step (2) is not straightforward, but Varagnolo–Vasserot
[
VV10
]
gave a brilliant definition for a
quantum radial parts map
.
Namely, the equivariance of
\mathcal{A}_{t}
ensures that it acts on certain spaces of intertwiners, and Etingof–Kirillov have identified the weighted traces of these intertwiners with Macdonald polynomials
[
EK94
]
.
We perform step (3) first for the case
t=q^{k}
, wherein we use work of Jordan
[
Jor14
]
to perform a classical degeneration
q\mapsto 1
.
From here, we can bootstrap to general
t
.

1.1.
Further directions

While it is unclear to us if a geometric story as in the rational case can be repeated here, the multiplicative setting is interesting due to its relation to character varieties for the torus.
In
[
AS96
]
as well as the more recent
[
BZBJ18a
,
BZBJ18b
]
,
\mathcal{A}_{t}
has been realized as a quantized character variety.
We have added an appendix that tracks down how the
SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-action of the DAHA is manifested in
\mathcal{A}_{t}
, which may be interesting from a topological perspective.
Let us note the similarities to conjectures of Morton–Samuelson
[
MS21
]
concering DAHAs and skeins (proved in
[
BCMN23
]
).

Shortly after the initial posting of this paper, we received the extremely interesting work
[
GJV23
]
, which initiates a quantum analogue of Springer theory through the beautiful idea of
q
-deforming the Hotta–Kashiwara
D
-module
[
HK84
]
.
In type
A
, the authors are indeed able to relate their construction to Weyl group representations.
Critical to this result is the isomorphism between
\mathcal{A}_{q}
and a spherical DAHA via Jordan’s
elliptic Schur–Weyl duality

[
Jor09
,
JV21
]
, which is only available in type
A
.
On the other hand, we can also obtain such an isomorphism via the radial parts map at
t=q
, wherein both algebras act on characters.
This approach generalizes to other types, although significant challenges remain in establishing such an isomorphism.

1.2.
Acknowledgements

This work received support from NSF-RTG grant “Algebraic Geometry and Representation Theory at Northeastern University” (DMS-1645877).

2.
Double affine Hecke algebras

In this section, we review the
GL_{n}
DAHA and associated structures.
Our main reference is
[
Che05
]
, although in order to make better contact with Etingof–Kirillov theory, we follow the conventions from
[
Kir97
]
.

2.1.
Definition

Let
R:=\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1},t^{\pm 1}]
and
K:=\mathbb{C}(q,t)
.
The
GL_{n}
-DAHA
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
is the
K
-algebra with generators

\{T_{i},X_{j}^{\pm 1},\pi^{\pm 1}\,|\,i=1,\ldots,n-1\hbox{ and }j=1,\ldots,n\}

and relations

\displaystyle(T_{i}-t)(T_{i}+t^{-1})=0;

\displaystyle X_{i}X_{j}=X_{j}X_{i};

\displaystyle T_{i}T_{i+1}T_{i}=T_{i+1}T_{i}T_{i+1};

\displaystyle T_{i}T_{j}=T_{j}T_{i}\hbox{ for }j\not=i,i+1;

\displaystyle T_{i}X_{j}=X_{j}T_{i}\hbox{ for }j\not=i,i+1;

\displaystyle T_{i}X_{i}T_{i}=X_{i+1};

\displaystyle\pi T_{i}=T_{i+1}\pi;

\displaystyle\pi^{n}T_{i}=T_{i}\pi^{n};

\displaystyle\pi X_{i}=X_{i+1}\pi;

\displaystyle\pi X_{n}=q^{-2}X_{1}\pi

We can also define this as an
R
-algebra, which we denote by
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}^{R}_{n}(q,t)
.

2.1.1.
Y-generators

The elements

(2.1)

Y_{i}:=T_{i}\cdots T_{n-1}\pi^{-1}T_{1}^{-1}\cdots T_{i-1}^{-1}

for
i=1,\ldots,n
generate a polynomial subalgebra.
They furnish an alternative presentation of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
, now with generators

\left\{T_{i},X_{j}^{\pm 1},Y_{j}^{\pm 1}\,|\,i=1,\ldots,n-1\hbox{ and }j=1,\ldots,n\right\}

and relations

(2.2)

\displaystyle(T_{i}-t)(T_{i}+t^{-1})=0;

\displaystyle T_{i}T_{i+1}T_{i}=T_{i+1}T_{i}T_{i+1};

\displaystyle T_{i}T_{j}=T_{j}T_{i}\hbox{ for }j\not=i,i+1;

\displaystyle X_{i}X_{j}=X_{j}X_{i};

\displaystyle Y_{i}Y_{j}=Y_{j}Y_{i};

\displaystyle T_{i}X_{i}T_{i}=X_{i+1}\hbox{ for }i\not=n;

\displaystyle T_{i}^{-1}Y_{i}T_{i}^{-1}=Y_{i+1}\hbox{ for }i\not=n;

\displaystyle T_{i}X_{j}=X_{j}T_{i}\hbox{ for }j\not=i,i+1;

\displaystyle T_{i}Y_{j}=Y_{j}T_{i}\hbox{ for }j\not=i,i+1;

\displaystyle Y_{1}\cdots Y_{n}X_{j}=q^{2}X_{j}Y_{1}\cdots Y_{n};

\displaystyle X_{1}\cdots X_{n}Y_{j}=q^{-2}Y_{j}X_{1}\cdots X_{n};

\displaystyle X_{1}Y_{2}=Y_{2}T^{2}_{1}X_{1}

We note that this presentation is also valid for
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)
.
From these relations, we can define a bigrading on
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)
by setting:

\displaystyle\deg(X_{j}^{\pm 1})

\displaystyle=(\pm 1,0)

\displaystyle\deg(Y_{j}^{\pm 1})

\displaystyle=(0,\pm 1)

\displaystyle\deg(T_{i})

\displaystyle=(0,0)

2.1.2.
Symmetrizer

The subalgebra
\mathcal{H}_{n}(t)
of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
generated by
T_{1},\ldots,T_{n-1}
is isomorphic to the usual Hecke algebra for the symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
with parameter
t
.
As such, one can make sense of elements
T_{w}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}(t)
for any
w\in\Sigma_{n}
.
Specifically, first let
s_{i}\in\Sigma_{n}
be the
i
th adjacent transposition.
For any reduced expression
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{a}}
, the element

T_{w}:=T_{i_{1}}\cdots T_{i_{a}}

is independent of the reduced expression.
Letting
\ell(w)=a
denote the length, define the symmetrizer

\tilde{\mathbf{s}}:=\sum_{w\in\Sigma_{n}}t^{\ell(w)}T_{w}

It will be useful to note the following:

(2.3)

\displaystyle T_{i}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}=\tilde{\mathbf{s}}T_{i}

\displaystyle=t\tilde{\mathbf{s}}\hbox{ for all $i$}

\displaystyle\sum_{w\in\Sigma_{n}}s^{\ell(w)}

\displaystyle=[n]_{s}!

(2.4)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{s}}^{2}

\displaystyle=[n]_{t^{2}}!\tilde{\mathbf{s}}

where

[\pm k]_{s}=\frac{1-s^{\pm k}}{1-s^{\pm 1}},\,[k]_{s}!=[k]_{s}[k-1]_{s}\cdots[1]_{s}

for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
From (
2.4
),

\mathbf{s}:=\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}}{[n]_{t^{2}}!}

is an idempotent.

2.1.3.
Triangular decomposition

For a vector
v=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, denote the monomial

Y^{v}:=Y_{1}^{v_{1}}\cdots Y_{n}^{v_{n}}

The following is Theorem 2.3(ii) of
[
Che95
]
:

Theorem 2.1
.

Any
H\in\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)
can be uniquely written as

(2.5)

H=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\Sigma_{n}\\
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\end{subarray}}Y^{v}f_{v,w}(X^{\pm 1}_{1},\ldots,X^{\pm 1}_{n})T_{w}

for some Laurent polynomials
\{f_{v,w}\}
with coefficients in
R
.

Corollary 2.2
.

\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)
is a free
R
-module.

2.2.
Spherical DAHA

The
spherical subalgebra

\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
is defined as

\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t):=\mathbf{s}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)\mathbf{s}\subset\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)

We denote the localizations
\tilde{R}:=R[(1-t^{2k})^{-1}]_{k>0}
and
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}^{\tilde{R}}_{n}(q,t):=\tilde{R}\otimes\ddot{\mathcal{H}}^{R}_{n}(q,t)
.
Note that
\mathbf{s}\in\ddot{\mathcal{H}}^{\tilde{R}}_{n}(q,t)
, and we define

\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t):=\mathbf{s}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\tilde{R}}(q,t)\mathbf{s}

We then make sense of the specialization
t=q^{k}
by setting

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,q^{k})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)\bigg|_{t=q^{k}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}(q,q^{k})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{C}(q)\otimes\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,q^{k})

2.2.1.
Bigrading revisited

Multiplying both sides of (
2.5
) by
\mathbf{s}
and absorbing the
T_{w}
into
\mathbf{s}
using (
2.3
), we can see that
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)
is spanned by elements of the form

\mathbf{s}\left(\sum_{v\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}Y^{v}f(X_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,X_{n}^{\pm 1})\right)\mathbf{s}

For such an element as above that is homogeneous with respect to the bigrading, we can see from (
2.2
) that its bidegree can be discerned by commuting with

(2.6)

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}\mathbf{X}_{n}^{\pm 1}\mathbf{s}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{s}X_{1}^{\pm 1}\cdots X_{n}^{\pm 1}\mathbf{s}

\displaystyle\hbox{ and }\mathbf{s}\mathbf{Y}_{n}^{\pm 1}\mathbf{s}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{s}Y_{1}^{\pm 1}\cdots Y_{n}^{\pm 1}\mathbf{s}

Proposition 2.3
.

H\in\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)
has bidegree
(a,b)
if and only if

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{s}\mathbf{X}_{n}\mathbf{s}\right)H\left(\mathbf{s}\mathbf{X}_{n}^{-1}\mathbf{s}\right)

\displaystyle=q^{-2a}H

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{s}\mathbf{Y}_{n}\mathbf{s}\right)H\left(\mathbf{s}\mathbf{Y}_{n}^{-1}\mathbf{s}\right)

\displaystyle=q^{2b}H

Let
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)_{\textrm{IV}}\subset\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)
denote the subalgebra generated by

\{T_{i},X_{j},Y^{-1}_{j}\,|\,i=1,\ldots,n-1\hbox{ and }j=1,\ldots,n\}

i.e. we restrict to positive powers of
X
-generators and negative powers of
Y
-generators (the “fourth quadrant” in the bigrading).
We then set

\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)_{\textrm{IV}}:=\mathbf{s}\left(\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)_{\textrm{IV}}\right)\mathbf{s}\subset\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)

The subalgebras
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)_{\mathrm{IV}}
,
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,q^{k})_{\mathrm{IV}}
, and
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})_{\mathrm{IV}}
are defined similarly.
Let
\mathcal{S}^{R}_{\mathrm{IV}}[a,b]
denote the homogeneous piece of
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)_{\mathrm{IV}}
of bidegree
(a,b)
and likewise for

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{t,\mathrm{IV}}[a,b]

\displaystyle\subset\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)_{\mathrm{IV}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{q^{k},\mathrm{IV}}[a,b]

\displaystyle\subset\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})_{\mathrm{IV}}

Finally, let

\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n}]:=\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}]

The symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
acts on
\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n}]
by permuting subscripts.

\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n}]
is also bigraded, where
\deg(x_{i})=(1,0)
and
\deg(y_{i})=(0,1)
.
Denote by
\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n}]^{\Sigma_{n}}_{a,b}
the subspace of invariant homogeneous elements of bidegree
(a,b)
.

Proposition 2.4
.

We have

\dim_{K}\mathcal{S}_{t,\mathrm{IV}}[a,-b]=\dim_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathcal{S}_{q^{k},\mathrm{IV}}[a,-b]=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n}]^{\Sigma_{n}}_{a,b}

Proof.

\mathcal{S}^{R}_{\mathrm{IV}}[a,b]
is a direct summand of the free
R
-module
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)_{\mathrm{IV}}
(Corollary
2.2
).
Therefore, it is also free, and the dimensions of
\mathcal{S}_{t,\mathrm{IV}}[a,b]
and
\mathcal{S}_{q^{k},\mathrm{IV}}[a,b]
are both equal to its rank.
To compute this rank, we can set
q=t=1
, in which case

\displaystyle\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)_{\mathrm{IV}}\big|_{q=t=1}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n}]\rtimes\mathbb{C}[\Sigma_{n}]

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)_{\mathrm{IV}}\big|_{q=t=1}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n}]^{\Sigma_{n}}

where
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
are the images of
X_{i}
and
Y_{i}^{-1}
, respectively.
∎

2.2.2.
Generators

We use ideas from the proofs of Proposition 2.5 of
[
SV11
]
and Lemma 5.2 of
[
FFJ+11
]
to produce nice generating sets.
For
r=1,\ldots,n
, Let
e_{r}
denote the
r
th elementary symmetric polynomial in
n
variables and set

(2.7)

\displaystyle e_{r}(\mathbf{X}_{n}^{\pm 1})

\displaystyle:=e_{r}(X_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,X_{n}^{\pm 1})

\displaystyle e_{r}(\mathbf{Y}_{n}^{\pm 1})

\displaystyle:=e_{r}(Y_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,Y_{n}^{\pm 1})

Lemma 2.5

(
[
VV10
, Lemma A.15.2]
)
.

We have the following:

(1)

\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)_{\mathrm{IV}}
is generated by

(2.8)

\left\{\mathbf{s}e_{r}(\mathbf{X}_{n})\mathbf{s},\mathbf{s}e_{r}(\mathbf{Y}_{n}^{-1})\mathbf{s}\,\middle|\,r=1,\ldots,n\right\}

and
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
is generated by the set (
2.8
) along with
\mathbf{s}\mathbf{X}_{n}^{-1}\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{s}\mathbf{Y}_{n}\mathbf{s}
.

(2)

The analogous statement holds for
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})_{\mathrm{IV}}
and
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})
for
k>2n
.

Proof.

For
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, let

P_{a,-b}=\mathbf{s}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{a}Y_{i}^{-b}\right)\mathbf{s}

First note that by a classical theorem of Weyl
[
Wey39
]
, the invariant polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n}]^{\Sigma_{n}}
is generated by power sums of the form:

p_{a,b}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{a}y_{i}^{b}=P_{a,-b}\bigg|_{q=t=1}

Moreover, by the identity

p_{a,b}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i-1}e_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})p_{a-i,b}

the
p_{a,b}
with
0\leq a\leq n
generate
\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y})_{n}]^{\Sigma_{n}}
.
Thus, the
P_{a,b}
with
0\leq a\leq n
generate
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)_{\mathrm{IV}}
modulo the ideal
(q-1,t-1)
.
By applying Nakayama’s Lemma to each (finite rank) bigraded piece, we get that they generate
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
and
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})
.

Next, note that
P_{1,0}=\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{X}_{n})\mathbf{s}
and
P_{0,-1}=\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{Y}_{n}^{-1})\mathbf{s}
.
By (
2.1
),
Y_{i}
becomes the
q^{2}
-shift operator on
X_{i}
at
t=1
.
We thus have

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}\left(P_{1,0}\right)^{a}\cdot P_{0,-1}

\displaystyle=(1-q^{-2})^{a}P_{a,-1}

(2.9)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}\left(P_{0,-1}\right)^{b}\cdot P_{a,-1}

\displaystyle=(q^{-2a}-1)^{b}P_{a,-1-b}

where
\mathrm{ad}\left(X\right)
is the adjoint operator:

\mathrm{ad}\left(X\right)=[X,-]

In the case of
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
, we have that after localizing to
\mathbb{C}(q)[t^{\pm 1}]
, any
P_{a,-b}
with
a,b>0
can be written in terms of
\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{X}_{n})\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{Y}_{n}^{-1})\mathbf{s}
modulo the ideal
(t-1)
.
We include the other elementary symmetric polynomials to cover the cases
a
or
b=0
.
The result follows by again apply Nakayama’s Lemma to each bigraded piece.
For
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})
, we need to specialize
q=e^{2\pi i/k}
in order to have
t=1
.
Equation (
2.9
) becomes problematic once
a\geq 2k
, but we obtain all
0\leq a\leq n
once
k>2n
.
∎

2.3.
Polynomial representation

While Lemma
2.5
gives us generators for
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
and
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})
, we do not have relations; in compensation, we have instead a faithful representation.

2.3.1.
Demazure-Lusztig operators

Let
K[\mathbf{x}_{n}^{\pm}]:=K[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots x_{n}^{\pm 1}]
be the ring of Laurent polynomials in
n
variables.
We similarly use the abbreviation
R[\mathbf{x}_{n}^{\pm}]
.
The symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
acts on both rings by permuting variables, and as before, we let
s_{i}\in\Sigma_{n}
denote the usual adjacent transposition.

Theorem 2.6

(
[
Che95
]
Theorem 2.3)
.

The following defines a faithful represention
\mathfrak{r}
of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)
on
R[\mathbf{x}_{n}^{\pm}]
: for
f\in K[\mathbf{x}_{n}^{\pm}]
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}(T_{i})

\displaystyle=ts_{i}+(t-t^{-1})\frac{s_{i}-1}{x_{i}/x_{i+1}-1}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}(X_{i})f

\displaystyle=x_{i}f

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}(\pi)f(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=f(x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,x_{n},q^{-2}x_{1})

This representation remains faithful on any specialization of
(q,t)
so long as
q
is not sent to a root of unity.

We will abuse notation and use
\mathfrak{r}
to also denote its versions obtained via base-change from
R
.
From the formula for
\mathfrak{r}(T_{i})
, we can see that

f\in K[\mathbf{x}_{n}^{\pm}]^{\Sigma_{n}}\hbox{ if and only if }\mathfrak{r}(T_{i})f=tf

for all
i
.
It follows from (
2.3
) that the restriction of
\mathfrak{r}
to
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
and
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{R}(q,t)
preserves the subring
\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(q,t):=K[\mathbf{x}_{n}^{\pm}]^{\Sigma_{n}}
.
We similarly define
\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(q)
in the obvious way.

The following is well known:

Proposition 2.7
.

The elements
\mathbf{s}e_{r}(\mathbf{X}_{n}^{\pm 1})\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{s}e_{r}(\mathbf{Y}_{n}^{\pm 1})\mathbf{s}
act on
f\in\Lambda^{\pm}_{n}(q,t)
by:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathbf{s}e_{r}(\mathbf{X}_{n}^{\pm 1})\mathbf{s}\right)f

\displaystyle=e_{r}(x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm 1})f

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathbf{s}e_{r}(\mathbf{Y}_{n}^{\pm 1})\mathbf{s}\right)f

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}}\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\not\in I\end{subarray}}\frac{t^{2}\left(\dfrac{x_{i}}{x_{j}}\right)^{\pm 1}-1}{\left(\dfrac{x_{i}}{x_{j}}\right)^{\pm 1}-1}\right)\prod_{i\in I}T_{q^{2},x_{i}}^{\pm 1}f

where
T_{q^{2},x_{i}}f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i},\ldots,x_{n})=f(x_{1},\ldots,q^{2}x_{i},\ldots,x_{n})
.

2.3.2.
Macdonald polynomials

We will need a nice basis for the representation of
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
on
\Lambda^{\pm}_{n}(q,t)
.
A natural starting point is the basis of
monomial symmetric functions
: for
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,

m_{\lambda}:=\sum_{w\in\Sigma_{n}/\mathrm{Stab}(\lambda)}x^{w\lambda}

where
\Sigma_{n}
acts on
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
by permutations and for
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,

x^{\mu}=x_{1}^{\mu_{1}}x_{2}^{\mu_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\mu_{n}}

We say that
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
is
dominant
if
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}
; it is easy to see that the
m_{\lambda}
for dominant
\lambda
provide a basis of
\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(q,t)
.

Theorem 2.8
.

We have the following:

(1)

For each dominant
\lambda
, there exists a unique
P_{\lambda}(q,t)\in\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(q,t)
satisfying

•

for
f\in\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(q,t)
,

\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathbf{s}f(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})\mathbf{s}\right)P_{\lambda}(q,t)=f\left(q^{2\lambda_{1}}t^{n-1},q^{2\lambda_{2}}t^{n-3},\ldots,q^{2\lambda_{n}}t^{1-n}\right)P_{\lambda}(q,t)

•

the coefficient of
m_{\lambda}
is
1
.

Moreover,
\left\{P_{\lambda}(q,t)\right\}
form a basis of
\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(q,t)
.

(2)

For any integer
k>0
,
P_{\lambda}(q,t)
can be specialized to
t=q^{k}
.

Proof.

Part (1) is classical.
For (2), one can consider the
tableaux sum formula
for
P_{\lambda}(q,t)
(cf.
[
Mac15
]
VI.6).
∎

P_{\lambda}(q,t)
is the
Macdonald polynomial
associated to
\lambda
.

Remark 2.9
.

When
\lambda
is a partition (i.e.,
\lambda_{n}\geq 0
), our
P_{\lambda}(q,t)
is actually the
P_{\lambda}(q^{2},t^{2})
found in
[
Mac15
]
.
As stated in the start of this section, we make this choice to better align with the Etingof-Kirillov approach to Macdonald polynomials.

3.
Quantum groups

3.1.
Quantum enveloping algebra

In this subsection, we review the algebra
\mathcal{U}:=U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
and some of its basic structures.

3.1.1.
Roots and weights

Let
\epsilon_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the
i
th coordinate vector.
The
root system

R
is the set
\left\{\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{j}\right\}
, and the set of
positive roots

R^{+}
is the subset where
i<j
.
Within
R^{+}
, we call the
n-1
elements

\alpha_{i}=\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{i+1}\hbox{ for }i=1,\ldots,n-1

the
simple roots
.
The
root lattice

Q
is the lattice spanned by
R
, and we denote by
Q^{+}
the semigroup generated by
R^{+}
.
We equip
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the usual symmetric pairing
\langle-,-\rangle
for which
\left\{\epsilon_{i}\right\}
is an orthonormal basis.

The set of
weights
is the subset

\left\{\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,|\,\langle\omega,\alpha\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\hbox{ for all }\alpha\in R\right\}

For
i=1,\ldots,n
, we let

\omega_{i}:=\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}+\cdots+\epsilon_{i}

denote the
i
th fundamental weight
.
The lattice spanned by
\left\{\omega_{i}\right\}
is called the
weight lattice

P
, which is equal to the lattice spanned by
\left\{\epsilon_{i}\right\}
.
For
\lambda,\mu\in P
, we order
\lambda\geq\mu
if
\lambda-\mu\in Q^{+}
.
A weight is
dominant
if it pairs nonnegatively with all roots.
Let
P^{+}\subset P
denote the subset of dominant elements.
This is
not
the set of dominant weights but rather the subset of dominant weights whose coefficient for
\omega_{n}
is an integer.
Finally, we set

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle:=\left(\frac{n-1}{2},\frac{n-3}{2},\ldots,\frac{1-n}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle:=\left(n-1,n-2,\ldots,0\right)

Both satisfy
\langle\rho,\alpha_{i}\rangle=\langle\delta,\alpha_{i}\rangle=1
for all
i
and
\langle\rho,\omega_{n}\rangle=\langle\delta,\omega_{n}\rangle=0
.
Note that
2\rho\in P
but
\rho\not\in P
if
n
is even.

3.1.2.
Definition

\mathcal{U}
is the
\mathbb{C}(q)
-algebra with generators

\left\{E_{i},F_{i},q^{h}\,|\,i=1,\ldots,n-1\hbox{ and }h\in P\right\}

and relations

\displaystyle q^{\vec{0}}=1,\,q^{h_{1}}q^{h_{2}}=q^{h_{1}+h_{2}},

\displaystyle q^{h}E_{i}q^{-h}=q^{\langle\alpha_{i},h\rangle}E_{i},\,q^{h}F_{i}q^{-h}=q^{-\langle\alpha_{i},h\rangle}F_{i},

\displaystyle E_{i}F_{j}-F_{j}E_{i}=\delta_{ij}\dfrac{q^{\alpha_{i}}-q^{-\alpha_{i}}}{q-q^{-1}},

\displaystyle E_{i}^{2}E_{i+1}-(q+q^{-1})E_{i}E_{i+1}E_{i}+E_{i}E_{i+1}^{2}=0,

\displaystyle F_{i}^{2}F_{i+1}-(q+q^{-1})F_{i}F_{i+1}F_{i}+F_{i}F_{i+1}^{2}=0,

where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta.
We can endow it with a Hopf algebra structure where the coproduct
\Delta
, counit
\epsilon
, and antipode
S
are given by

\displaystyle\Delta(E_{i})=E_{i}\otimes q^{\alpha_{i}}+1\otimes E_{i},\hskip 14.22636pt\Delta(F_{i})=F_{i}\otimes 1+q^{-\alpha_{i}}\otimes F_{i},\hskip 14.22636pt\Delta(q^{h})=q^{h}\otimes q^{h};

\displaystyle\epsilon(E_{i})=\epsilon(F_{i})=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\epsilon(q^{h})=1;

\displaystyle S(E_{i})=-E_{i}q^{-\alpha_{i}},\hskip 14.22636ptS(F_{i})=-q^{\alpha_{i}}F_{i},\hskip 14.22636ptS(q^{h})=q^{-h};

For coproducts, we will use
Sweedler notation
:

\Delta(x)=x_{(1)}\otimes x_{(2)}

With this Hopf algebra structure,
\mathcal{U}
acts on itself via the
adjoint
action: for
u,v\in\mathcal{U}
,

\mathrm{ad}_{v}u:=v_{(1)}uS(v_{(2)})

Finally, we will denote by
\mathcal{U}_{c}
the same algebra equipped with the co-opposite coalgebra structure.

3.2.
R-matrix

The
universal R-matrix

\mathcal{R}
is an invertible element in a suitable completion of
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 2}
.
We will vaguely write

\mathcal{R}=\sum_{s}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-11.38931pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.92825pt}}_{{\kern-6.33623pt{s}\kern 3.92825pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-11.38931pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.67627pt}}}\otimes\mathchoice{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}

and even employ an Einstein-like notation:
\mathcal{R}=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-11.38931pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.92825pt}}_{{\kern-6.33623pt{s}\kern 3.92825pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-11.38931pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.92825pt}}_{{\kern-6.33623pt{s}\kern 3.92825pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-7.70789pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.34679pt}}_{{\kern-4.09853pt{s}\kern 2.34679pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-7.03737pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.67627pt}}_{{\kern-3.42801pt{s}\kern 1.67627pt}}}\otimes\mathchoice{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}
.
Let
\mathcal{R}_{xy}
denote the tensor where
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-11.38931pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.92825pt}}_{{\kern-6.33623pt{s}\kern 3.92825pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-11.38931pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.92825pt}}_{{\kern-6.33623pt{s}\kern 3.92825pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-7.70789pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.34679pt}}_{{\kern-4.09853pt{s}\kern 2.34679pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-7.03737pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.67627pt}}_{{\kern-3.42801pt{s}\kern 1.67627pt}}}
is inserted in the
x
th tensorand and
\mathchoice{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}
is inserted in the
y
th tensorand, and let
b:\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 2}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 2}
denote the tensor flip.
The following equations distinguish
\mathcal{R}
:

(3.1)

\displaystyle(\Delta\otimes 1)\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23},\,(1\otimes\Delta)\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12},

(3.2)

\displaystyle b\Delta(h)=\mathcal{R}\Delta(h)\mathcal{R}^{-1}

Some consequences of (
3.1
) are:

(3.3)

\displaystyle(\epsilon\otimes 1)\mathcal{R}=(1\otimes\epsilon)\mathcal{R}=1

(3.4)

\displaystyle(S\otimes 1)\mathcal{R}=(1\otimes S)\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{-1}

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{12}\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23}=\mathcal{R}_{23}\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{12}

Equation (
3.5
) is called the
quantum Yang-Baxter equation
.
Finally, we recall a factorization of
\mathcal{R}
.
Let
\mathcal{U}^{+}
be the subspace of
\mathcal{U}
spanned by products of
\left\{E_{i}\right\}
and
\mathcal{U}^{-}
be the subspace spanned by products of
\left\{F_{i}\right\}
.
We then have

(3.6)

\mathcal{R}=q^{-\sum\epsilon_{i}\otimes\epsilon_{i}}(1\otimes 1+\mathcal{R}^{*})

where
\mathcal{R}^{*}
is an element of a suitable completion of
\mathcal{U}^{+}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{-}
.

\mathcal{R}
is an infinite sum, but its action on tensors of highest weight representations is well-defined.
For two representations
V
and
W
of
\mathcal{U}
, let
b_{V,W}:V\otimes W\rightarrow W\otimes V
be the tensor flip.
As a result of (
3.2
), we have that the composition

\beta_{V,W}:=b_{V,W}\mathcal{R}\big|_{V,W}:V\otimes W\rightarrow W\otimes V

is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{U}
-modules.
We note that

\beta_{V,W}^{-1}=b_{W,V}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{21}\big|_{W,V}

3.2.1.
Vector representation

Let
\mathbb{V}:=V_{\omega_{1}}\cong\mathbb{C}^{n}
be the vector representation of
\mathcal{U}
.
We denote by
E_{j}^{i}
the matrix unit sending

E_{j}^{i}e_{j}=e_{i}

The specialized R-matrix
R^{\prime}:=\mathcal{R}\big|_{\mathbb{V},\mathbb{V}}
has the form

R^{\prime}=q\sum_{i}E_{i}^{i}\otimes E_{i}^{i}+\sum_{i\not=j}E_{i}^{i}\otimes E_{j}^{j}+(q-q^{-1})\sum_{i<j}E_{i}^{j}\otimes E_{j}^{i}

In accordance with
[
Jor14
]
, we will more often work with the transposed version:

(3.7)

R=q\sum_{i}E_{i}^{i}\otimes E_{i}^{i}+\sum_{i\not=j}E_{i}^{i}\otimes E_{j}^{j}+(q-q^{-1})\sum_{i>j}E_{i}^{j}\otimes E_{j}^{i}

Remark 3.1
.

Because our R-matrix
R^{\prime}
, which is dictated by the coproduct
\Delta
, is different from that of
[
Jor14
]
, some of our formulas will differ from those of
loc. cit.
, e.g. (
4.8
).

3.2.2.
Symmetric and exterior powers

One can check that
R
satisfies the
Hecke condition
:

(3.8)

(\beta_{\mathbb{V},\mathbb{V}}-q)(\beta_{\mathbb{V},\mathbb{V}}+q^{-1})=0

In the tensor power
\mathbb{V}^{\otimes m}
, let
\mathbb{V}^{i}
denote the
i
th tensorand.
The Yang-Baxter equation (
3.5
) and the Hecke condition (
3.8
) imply that the map

T_{i}\mapsto\beta_{\mathbb{V}^{i},\mathbb{V}^{i+1}}

yields a representation of the
\Sigma_{m}
Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{m}(q)
on
\mathbb{V}^{\otimes m}
.
Similar to
2.1.2
, we can
q
-symmetrize and
q
-antisymmetrize by applying the operators

\sum_{w\in\Sigma_{m}}q^{\ell(w)}T_{w}\hbox{ and }\sum_{w\in\Sigma_{m}}(-q)^{-\ell(w)}T_{w}

respectively to
\mathbb{V}^{\otimes m}
.
The results are denoted by
S_{q}^{m}\mathbb{V}
and
\wedge_{q}^{m}\mathbb{V}
.
Since
\beta_{\mathbb{V},\mathbb{V}}
is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{U}
-modules, these symmetric and exterior powers are also
\mathcal{U}
-modules.

We call
\mathbb{1}_{1}:=\wedge^{n}_{q}\mathbb{V}^{n}
the
determinant representation
.
It is one-dimensional, and thus
\mathcal{U}
acts via a character that we denote by
\chi
:

(3.9)

\begin{gathered}\chi(E_{i})=\chi(F_{i})=0\\
\chi(q^{h})=q^{\langle h,\omega_{n}\rangle}\end{gathered}

The action on the tensor powers
\mathbb{1}_{k}:=(\wedge^{n}_{q}\mathbb{V})^{\otimes k}
is then via
\chi^{k}
.
We similarly define
\mathbb{1}_{-1}:=\wedge_{q}^{n}\mathbb{V}^{*}
and
\mathbb{1}_{-k}
.

3.2.3.
Drinfeld element

Define the
Drinfeld element
as

u:=m(S\otimes 1)\mathcal{R}_{21}=S(\mathchoice{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}})\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-11.38931pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.67627pt}}_{{\kern-3.42801pt{s}\kern 1.67627pt}}}

where
m
is the multiplication map.
This is an infinite sum defined in a suitable completion of
\mathcal{U}
.
The following is proved in
[
Dri89a
]
:

Proposition 3.2
.

The Drinfeld element

satisfies:

(3.10)

\displaystyle=m(S^{-1}\otimes \displaystyle S^{2}(x)

\displaystyle=uxu^{-1}

(3.12)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{R}_{12}\mathcal{R})^{-1}(u\otimes u)

3.3.
Representations

We now turn our attention to the category
\mathcal{C}
of finite-dimensional
\mathcal{U}
-modules with weights lying in
P
.

\mathcal{C}
is semisimple with simple objects indexed by
P^{+}
; for
\lambda\in P^{+}
, we denote the corresponding irreducible representation by
V_{\lambda}
.
Since
\mathcal{U}
is a Hopf algebra,
\mathcal{C}
is a monoidal category.
The constructions from the previous subsection involving the R-matrix endow
\mathcal{C}
with the structure of a
ribbon category
.
As worked out in
[
RT90
]
, such categories come with a graphical calculus for working with morphisms.
Here, we will review this calculus as presented in Section 2.3 of
[
BK01
]
.

3.3.1.
Arrows

We will depict a morphism
f:V\rightarrow W
of
\mathcal{U}
-modules as an upward oriented arrow decorated with a coupon marked by
f
.
When
V=W
and
f
is the identity, we will omit the coupon:

Tensor product of objects and morphisms will be denoted by horizontal juxtaposition:

3.3.2.
Duality

For
V\in\mathcal{C}
, we endow
V^{*}
with the structure of a
\mathcal{U}
-module via

x\cdot f(v)=f(S(x)v)

for
x\in\mathcal{U}
,
f\in V^{*}
, and
v\in V
.
We set
(V\otimes W)^{*}=W^{*}\otimes V^{*}
as they are isomorphic
\mathcal{U}
-modules under the natural tensor flip.
In our graphical calculus, we will denote
V^{*}
using
V
but use
downward
pointing arrows.
Note that
V
and
V^{**}
are isomorphic under the
nontrivial
isomorphism
S^{2}
.
It is easy to see that

S^{2}(x)=\mathrm{ad}_{q^{2\rho}}(x)

Since
\Delta(q^{2\rho})=q^{2\rho}\otimes q^{2\rho}
, we can use
q^{2\rho}:V\rightarrow V^{**}
to identify the two modules in a manner that respects tensor products.
We will do so and write
V
instead of
V^{**}
, which will always imply a twist by
q^{-2\rho}
.

3.3.3.
Evaluation and coevaluation

Let
\left\{v_{i}\right\}
be a basis of
V
with corresponding dual basis
\left\{v^{i}\right\}
and let
\mathbb{1}\in\mathcal{C}
be the trivial representation.
The canonical maps

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\mapsto cv_{i}\otimes v^{i}\in V\otimes V^{*}

\displaystyle V^{*}\otimes V\ni v^{i}\otimes v_{j}

\displaystyle\mapsto\delta_{ij}

are homomorphisms
\mathrm{coev}_{V}:\mathbb{1}\rightarrow V\otimes V^{*}
and
\mathrm{ev}_{V}:V^{*}\otimes V\rightarrow\mathbb{1}
, respectively.
If
V
is irreducible, they are the unique such homomorphisms.
Graphically, we will omit depicting
\mathbb{1}
;
\mathrm{ev}_{V}
and
\mathrm{coev}_{V}
appear as caps and cups oriented towards the left:

The ordering of tensor factors matter in
\mathrm{ev}_{V}
and
\mathrm{coev}_{V}
.
To define maps with the opposite ordering, we will use
q^{2\rho}
to identify
V
and
V^{**}
.

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\mapsto cv^{i}\otimes q^{-2\rho}v_{i}\in V^{*}\otimes V

\displaystyle V\otimes V^{*}\ni q^{-2\rho}v_{i}\otimes v^{j}

\displaystyle\mapsto\delta_{ij}

We denote these maps by
\mathrm{qcoev}_{V}
and
\mathrm{qev}_{V}
, respectively.
Graphically, they will be depicted as cups and caps with orientations opposite from before:

3.3.4.
Adjunction

The caps and cups allow us to define (right) adjoints: for
f:V\rightarrow W
,
f^{*}:W^{*}\rightarrow V^{*}
is given by

f^{*}:=(\mathrm{ev}_{W}\otimes 1_{V^{*}})(1_{W^{*}}\otimes f\otimes 1_{V^{*}})(1_{W^{*}}\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{V})

Note that the adjoint of the identity map of
V
is the identity map of
V^{*}
.

3.3.5.
Braiding

We will depict
\beta_{V,W}
and
\beta_{V,W}^{-1}
as braid crossings:

The quantum Yang-Baxter equation (
3.5
) implies that
\beta_{V,W}
endows
\mathcal{C}
with the structure of a braided monoidal category.

3.3.6.
Ribbon structure

By (
3.11
),
u
gives an isomorphism
V\rightarrow V^{**}
and thus
q^{-2\rho}u
is an automorphism of
V
, i.e. it is central.
We define the
ribbon element

\nu
by

\nu:=\left(q^{-2\rho}u\right)^{-1}=u^{-1}q^{2\rho}

Formula (
3.12
) implies that
\nu
satisfies

(3.13)

\Delta(\nu)=\mathcal{R}_{21}\mathcal{R}(\nu\otimes\nu)

The following was computed in
[
Dri89a
]
:

Proposition 3.3
.

On
V_{\lambda}
,
u
acts as
q^{-\langle\lambda,\lambda+2\rho\rangle}q^{2\rho}
.
Thus, the ribbon element
\nu
acts by the scalar
q^{\langle\lambda,\lambda+2\rho\rangle}
.

Using (
3.10
), we can see that
\nu
can be drawn in two ways:

It is not quite true that morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
only depend on the isotopy type of its diagram in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
under the graphical calculus.
Rather, we should view each strand as a ribbon with a front side and back side, and we require the front side to always face the reader at the start and end of the ribbon, .
The back side may appear in the middle if the loop given by
\nu
appears, in which case the ribbon in twisted twice.
Our graphical calculus assigns to each morphism in
\mathcal{C}
a
\mathcal{C}
-colored ribbon tangle
.

Theorem 3.4

(
[
RT90
]
)
.

A morphism in
\mathcal{C}
only depends on the isotopy type of its associated tangle.

We refer the reader to the original source as well as
[
BK01
]
for details.
In practice, we will instead work with strands but keep track of loops representing
\nu
.

3.4.
Reflection equation algebra

Here, we introduce a quantization of the Hopf algebra of functions on
GL_{n}
.
We would like this Hopf algebra to be a
\mathcal{U}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
-module, and, critically, we want its structure maps to be
\mathcal{U}
-homomorphisms.
This requires a braided variant of Tannakian reconstruction, defined by Majid
[
Maj93
]
.
The resulting algebra is a localization of what is known as the
reflection equation algebra
.

3.4.1.
Majid reconstruction

Recall that
\mathcal{C}
is the category of finite-dimensional
\mathcal{U}
-modules with weights in
P
.
We define the
\mathcal{U}
-module
\mathcal{O}
as a space of matrix elements:

(3.14)

\mathcal{O}:=\left(\bigoplus_{V\in\mathcal{C}}V^{*}\otimes V\right)\bigg/\left\langle f^{*}(w^{*})\otimes v-w^{*}\otimes f(v)\,\big|\,v\in V,w^{*}\in W^{*},f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{GL_{n}}(V,W)\right\rangle

For the categorically-minded,
\mathcal{O}
is the
coend
of the identity functor of
\mathcal{C}
.
By considering for
f
in (
3.14
) the projections onto irreducible representations, we obtain an analogue of the Peter–Weyl Theorem:

(3.15)

\mathcal{O}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P^{+}}V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda}

As we will see below, we can use operations on representations to define a Hopf algebra structure on
\mathcal{O}
that recovers at
q=1
the classical Hopf algebra structure on the ring of functions on
GL_{n}
.

•

Coalgebra structure:

The coalgebra structure is identical to that of the classical case.
For
v^{*}\otimes v\in V^{*}\otimes V
, we define the coproduct
\nabla(v^{*}\otimes v)
as

\nabla(v^{*}\otimes v)=v^{*}\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{V}(1)\otimes v

The evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}_{V}
on
V^{*}\otimes V
yields the counit.

•

Algebra structure:

In the classical case, the product structure entails permuting tensorands.
To make such an operation a
\mathcal{U}
-morphism, we utilize the braiding.
For
v^{*}\otimes v\in V^{*}\otimes V
and
w^{*}\otimes w\in W^{*}\otimes W
,

(3.16)

m(v^{*}\otimes v\otimes w^{*}\otimes w)=\mathchoice{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76944pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.26389pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{t}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76944pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.26389pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{t}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76944pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.26389pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{t}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76944pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.26389pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{t}}}}\mathchoice{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}w^{*}\otimes\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76944pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.26389pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{t}}}}r^{{\kern-9.70157pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76944pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.26389pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern The inclusion
\mathbb{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{1}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{1}\in\mathcal{O}
provides the unit.

•

Antipode:
The antipode
\iota
is given by

(3.17)

\iota(v^{*}\otimes 3.92825pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-7.70789pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.34679pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-7.03737pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.67627pt}}_{{\kern-3.42801pt{s}\kern 1.67627pt}}}v^{*}

We note that the relations for the coend (
3.14
) are necessary to show that
\iota
is an antipode.
For example, in computing
m\circ(1\otimes\iota)\circ\nabla
, we use the coend relation in the first equality below:

3.4.2.
Generating matrix

Since the finite-dimensional representions of
\mathcal{U}
can be built out of tensor functors applied to the vector representation
\mathbb{V}
, it is perhaps unsurprising that
\mathcal{O}
has a presentation written in terms of matrix elements of
\mathbb{V}
.
The generators of this presentation are a set of symbols
\left\{m_{j}^{i}\,|\,i,j=1,\ldots n\right\}
.
We arrange them into a matrix
M=(M_{j}^{i})
where

M^{i}_{j}=E_{j}^{i}\otimes m^{i}_{j}

Definition 3.5
.

The
reflection equation algebra

\mathfrak{R}
is generated by
\left\{m^{i}_{j}\right\}
and has relations

(3.18)

R_{21}M_{13}R_{12}M_{23}=M_{23}R_{21}M_{13}R_{12}

Theorem 3.6

(
[
DM03
]
)
.

The map
m_{j}^{i}\mapsto e^{i}\otimes e_{j}
gives an embedding from
\mathfrak{R}
to
\mathcal{O}
.
It is an isomorphism after inverting a central element of
\mathfrak{R}
called the quantum determinant
\det_{q}(M)
, which is mapped to
\mathrm{qcoev}_{\mathbb{1}_{1}}(1)
.

Equation (
3.18
) is known as the
reflection equation
.
For an explicit formula for the quantum determinant, see
[
JW20
]
.

We will abuse notation and conflate
\mathfrak{R}
with its image in
\mathcal{O}
.
Let
M^{-1}:=(1\otimes\iota)(M)
.
By the definition of the antipode, we have:

M^{-1}M=MM^{-1}=I

Corollary 3.7
.

\mathcal{O}
is generated by
\det_{q}(M)
and the entries of
M^{-1}
.

Proof.

First, note that

\textstyle\iota(\det_{q}(M))=\iota^{-1}(\det_{q}(M))=\det_{q}(M)^{-1}

Thus, by writing
\det_{q}(M)
in terms of the entries of
M
and applying
\iota
, we obtain an expression for
\det_{q}(M)^{-1}
in terms of the entries of
M^{-1}
.
Similarly, we can write the entries of
(1\otimes\iota^{-1})(M)
in terms of the entries of
M
and
\det_{q}(M)^{-1}
.
Applying
\iota
, we obtain an expression for the entries of
M
in terms of those of
M^{-1}
and
\det_{q}(M)
.
∎

3.4.3.
Killing form

Matrix elements give functionals on
\mathcal{U}
in the natural way: for
v^{*}\otimes v\in V^{*}\otimes V
and
x\in\mathcal{U}
:

(v^{*}\otimes v)(x)=v^{*}(xv)

A quantum analogue of the Killing form would allow us to view matrix elements as sitting inside the enveloping algebra.
The canonical tensor for such a form is given by
\mathcal{R}_{21}\mathcal{R}
.

Theorem 3.8

(
[
JL92
]
)
.

The map
\kappa:\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
given by

\kappa(v^{*}\otimes v)=((v^{*}\otimes v)(-)\otimes 1)\mathcal{R}_{21}\mathcal{R}

is a
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant algebra embedding, where
\mathcal{U}
is endowed with the adjoint action.

Observe that the map
V^{*}\otimes V\otimes W\rightarrow W
given by

v^{*}\otimes v\otimes w\mapsto\kappa(v^{*}\otimes v)(w)

is the morphism in
\mathcal{C}
depicted by the following diagram:

We will depict
\kappa(\mathcal{O})
by replacing the strand for
W
above by a dotted line oriented upwards—a “ghost strand”.
Our graphical calculus is still valid for such strands because it holds for any choice of representation
W
“filling in” the ghost strand; by the quantum analogue of a theorem of Harish-Chandra
[
HC49
]
, any
x\in\mathcal{U}
that acts trivially on all
W\in\mathcal{C}
is necessarily zero.
Multiplying left-to-right in
\kappa(\mathcal{O})
corresponds to stacking ghost strands top-to-bottom.
For example, that
\kappa
is an algebra map follows from the manipulation below:

Finally, it can be useful to know that
\kappa\circ\iota
yields the opposite crossing:

Theorem
3.8
omits any statement about coalgebra structures because
\kappa
does not intertwine
\nabla
with
\Delta
.
By considering the action of
\kappa(v^{*}\otimes v)
on a tensor product of modules, we see that
\Delta\circ\kappa
merely doubles the dotted strand.
We can force an instance of
\kappa
for each dotted strand using
\nabla
and some braidings:

Properly comprehending the diagram on the right, we get:

Proposition 3.9

(
[
VV10
, Example 1.3.3(c)]
)
.

Let
\left\{v_{i}\right\}\subset V
and
\left\{v^{i}\right\}\subset V^{*}
be dual bases.
For
v^{*}\otimes v\in V^{*}\otimes V
, we have

(3.19)

(\Delta\circ\kappa)(v^{*}\otimes v)=\kappa(v^{*}\otimes In particular,
\kappa(\mathcal{O})
is a left coideal sublagebra of
\mathcal{U}
, i.e.

\displaystyle(\Delta\circ\kappa)(\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle\subset\mathcal{U}\otimes\kappa(\mathcal{O})

3.5.
Etingof–Kirillov theory

Equipped with a notion of functions on quantum
GL_{n}
, we move on to a realization of Macdonald polynomials as spherical functions on the quantum group.
This was discovered by Etingof and Kirillov, Jr.
[
EK94
]
.
We conclude this section by making contact with
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})
.

3.5.1.
Traces of intertwiners

Let
U\in\mathcal{C}
.
We will be concerned with the space of homomorphisms

I(V_{\lambda},U):=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{U}}(V_{\lambda},V_{\lambda}\otimes U)

also called
intertwiners
.
Let
v_{\lambda}\in V_{\lambda}
be a highest weight vector and
U[0]
denote the zero weight space.
For an intertwiner
\Phi
, set

\langle\Phi\rangle=(v_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes 1_{U})\Phi(v_{\lambda})\in U[0]

Proposition 3.10

(cf. 3.1 in
[
EL05
]
)
.

The map
\Phi\mapsto\langle\Phi\rangle
is an injective map
I(V_{\lambda},U)\hookrightarrow U[0]
.

To extract a Laurent polynomial from this, we take the weighted trace over
V_{\lambda}
:

\Phi\mapsto\varphi:=\mathrm{tr}_{V_{\lambda}}(\Phi(q^{2\mu}))

Viewing this as a function of
\mu\in P
and setting
x_{i}=q^{2\langle\epsilon_{i},-\rangle}
, we obtain a Laurent polynomial in the variables
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
valued in
U[0]
.
From Proposition
3.10
, it follows that the trace map is injective because the intertwiner is determined by the coefficient of
x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\lambda_{n}}
.

We would prefer to work instead with what can be called
U
-spherical functions on quantum
GL_{n}
.
This amounts to applying quantum coevaluation maps:

\displaystyle I(V_{\lambda},U)

\displaystyle\cong(V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda}\otimes U)^{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle\mapsto(1_{V_{\lambda}^{*}}\otimes\Phi)\circ(\mathrm{qcoev}_{V_{\lambda}})=:\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\cup}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-16.2728pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.08614pt{\cup}\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\cup}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-16.2728pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.08614pt{\cup}\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\cup}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-11.11665pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-5.98334pt{\cup}\kern 3.5389pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\cup}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-10.10553pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-4.97221pt{\cup}\kern 2.52777pt}}}

Since
q^{2\rho}
and
q^{2\mu}
are both grouplike, we have

(3.20)

\varphi=(\mathrm{ev}_{V_{\lambda}}\otimes 1_{U})\left((q^{-2\mu-2\rho}\otimes 1_{V_{\lambda}\otimes U})\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\cup}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-16.2728pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.08614pt{\cup}\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\cup}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-16.2728pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.08614pt{\cup}\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\cup}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-11.11665pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-5.98334pt{\cup}\kern 3.5389pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\cup}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-10.10553pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.3889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26666pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.33333pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-4.97221pt{\cup}\kern 2.52777pt}}}\right)

This has a clear graphical interpretation in terms of closing the loop between the
V_{\lambda}
strands, but we will refrain from drawing it as the insertion of
q^{-2\mu}
breaks
\mathcal{U}
-equivariance.

3.5.2.
Macdonald polynomials revisited

Now, fix
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
We will consider the case

U=U_{k}:=S_{q}^{n(k-1)}\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{-(k-1)}

U_{k}[0]
is one-dimensional, spanned by the vector whose tensorand in
S_{q}^{nk}\mathbb{V}
is the
q
-symmetrization of

\left(e_{1}\otimes e_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{n}\right)^{\otimes{k-1}}

Fixing a vector
u_{0}\in U_{k}[0]
, we can identify
U_{k}[0]
with
\mathbb{C}(q)
.
Recall that
\delta=(n-1,n-2,\ldots,0)
.

Theorem 3.11

(
[
EK94
]
)
.

We have the following:

(1)

For
\lambda\in P^{+}
,
I(V_{\lambda},U_{k})
is nonzero if and only if
\lambda-(k-1)\delta
is dominant.
For
\lambda\in P^{+}
, set

V_{\lambda}^{k}:=V_{\lambda+(k-1)\delta}

Thus, for
\lambda\in P^{+}
, there exists a unique nonzero intertwiner

\Phi^{k}_{\lambda}:V_{\lambda}^{k}\rightarrow V_{\lambda}^{k}\otimes U_{k}

with
\langle\Phi^{k}_{\lambda}\rangle=u_{0}
.

(2)

Let
\varphi^{k}_{\lambda}
be the weighted trace
\mathrm{tr}_{V_{\lambda}}(\Phi^{k}_{\lambda}(q^{2\mu}))
.
We have:

P_{\lambda}(q,q^{k})=\varphi^{k}_{\lambda}\big/\varphi^{k}_{0}

3.5.3.
Spherical DAHA generators

Recall the generators of
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})
given by Lemma
2.5
(cf. also (
2.6
) and (
2.7
)).
We have two natural actions of
\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{U}}
on the Macdonald polynomials.
Let
\mathrm{ch}_{V_{\pi}}
denote the character of
V_{\pi}
.
The first action is through insertion via
\kappa
:

Theorem 3.12

(
[
EK94
]
)
.

We have

\Phi^{k}_{\lambda}(\kappa(\mathrm{qcoev}_{V^{*}_{\pi}}(1))-)=q^{-|\pi|(n-1)}\mathrm{ch}_{V_{\pi}}(q^{2(\lambda+k\delta)})\Phi_{\lambda}^{k}

This induces the same action on the weighted trace.
In terms of spherical functions, this corresponds to:

(\kappa(\mathrm{qcoev}_{V_{\pi}}(1))\otimes 1_{V_{\lambda}\otimes 4.
Quantum differential operators

4.1.
Varagnolo–Vasserot double

Out of our quantum ring of functions
\mathcal{O}
, we would like to define a notion of quantum differential operators with very specific equivariance properties.
Let us catalogue the
\mathcal{U}
-actions on
\mathcal{O}
:

(1)

Left coregular action:

g\triangleright(v^{*}\otimes v)=gv^{*}\otimes v
(this is an action of the co-opposite
\mathcal{U}_{c}
);

(2)

Right coregular action:

g\triangleleft(v^{*}\otimes v)=v^{*}\otimes gv
;

(3)

Coadjoint action:

g\bowtie(v^{*}\otimes v)=g_{(1)}v^{*}\otimes g_{(2)}v
.

We have been mainly concerned with the coadjoint action, for which the Hopf algebra structure maps are homomorphisms.
Our goal is to construct a smash product between
\mathcal{O}
and
\kappa(\mathcal{O})
utilizing the left coregular action, but we would like all structure maps to be equivariant with respect to the coadjoint action on
\mathcal{O}
and the adjoint action on
\kappa(\mathcal{O})
.

4.1.1.
Drinfeld twist

The left and right coregular actions give an action of
\mathcal{U}_{c}\otimes\mathcal{U}
on
\mathcal{O}
.
However, the braidings in (
3.16
) prevent the product from being a
\mathcal{U}_{c}\otimes\mathcal{U}
-homomorphism.
This can be fixed by altering the coproduct of
\mathcal{U}_{c}\otimes\mathcal{U}
using Drinfeld’s twisting procedure
[
Dri89b
]
.
First, let
\Delta_{2}
denote the coproduct of
\mathcal{U}_{c}\otimes\mathcal{U}
:

\Delta_{2}(g\otimes h)=g_{(2)}\otimes h_{(1)}\otimes g_{(1)}\otimes h_{(2)}

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
denote the algebra
\mathcal{U}_{c}\otimes\mathcal{U}
endowed with coproduct

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}(g\otimes h)

\displaystyle:=(\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23})^{-1}\Delta_{2}(g\otimes h)(\mathcal{R}_{13}\mathcal{R}_{23})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{23}^{-1}(g_{(1)}\otimes h_{(1)}\otimes g_{(2)}\otimes h_{(2)})\mathcal{R}_{23}

and antipode

\widetilde{S}_{2}(g\otimes h)=\mathcal{R}_{21}\big(S(g)\otimes S(h)\big)\mathcal{R}_{21}^{-1}

With these structures,
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
is a Hopf algebra in a suitably completed sense.
We will use an embellished Sweedler notation for
\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}
:

\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}(g\otimes h)=\tilde{g}_{(1)}\otimes\tilde{h}_{(1)}\otimes\tilde{g}_{(2)}\otimes\tilde{h}_{(2)}

Proposition 4.1
.

The multiplication map
m
of
\mathcal{O}
is a
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
-homomorphism.

Proof.

This follows from the calculation:

\displaystyle m\bigg(\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}(g\otimes h)(v^{*}\otimes v\otimes w^{*}\otimes w)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\beta_{V^{*}\otimes V,W^{*}}\bigg(\mathcal{R}_{23}^{-1}(g_{(1)}v^{*}\otimes 4.1.2.
Three embeddings

Corresponding to the three actions of
\mathcal{U}
on
\mathcal{O}
, there are three embeddings of
\mathcal{U}
into
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
.
First, let us consider the subalgebras
\mathcal{U}\otimes 1
and
1\otimes\mathcal{U}
.
It is clear that the restrictions of the

to these two subalgebras respectively yield the left and right coregular actions.

Thus,
\mathcal{U}\otimes 1
is a left coideal subalgebra, and so by Theorem
3.8
and Proposition
3.9
,
\kappa(\mathcal{O})\otimes 1
is a left coideal subalgebra as well.

Next, view the coproduct as a map
\Delta:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
.
Observe that by (
3.1
),

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}\circ\Delta)(g)

\displaystyle=g_{(1)}\otimes g_{(2)}\otimes g_{(3)}\otimes g_{(4)}

(4.2)

\displaystyle(1\otimes\widetilde{S})(\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}\circ\Delta)(g)

\displaystyle=g_{(1)}\otimes g_{(2)}\otimes S(g_{(4)})\otimes S(g_{(3)})

From these equations, we can see that
\Delta
is in fact a Hopf algebra morphism.
Moreover, from (
4.2
), we can see that the adjoint action of
\Delta(\mathcal{U})
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
preserves
\mathcal{U}\otimes 1
, on which it acts by the usual adjoint action on
\mathcal{U}
.

4.1.3.
Smash product

Now consider the smash product
\mathcal{O}\rtimes\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
.
This is the tensor product
\mathcal{O}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
subject to the relation that for
v^{*}\otimes v\in\mathcal{O}
and
g\otimes h\in\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
,

(4.3)

(g\otimes h)(v^{*}\otimes v)=(\tilde{g}_{(1)}v^{*}\otimes\tilde{h}_{(1)}v)(\tilde{g}_{(2)}\otimes\tilde{h}_{(2)})

We will abuse notation and use
\mathcal{O}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
to denote
\mathcal{O}\otimes 1\otimes 1
and
1\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
, respectively.
Since
\kappa(\mathcal{O})\otimes 1\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
is a left coideal, the subspace
\mathcal{O}\rtimes(\kappa(\mathcal{O})\otimes 1)
is in fact a subalgebra.
We denote by
\partial_{\triangleright}
the embedding
\mathcal{O}\rightarrow 1\otimes\kappa(\mathcal{O})\otimes 1\subset\mathcal{O}\rtimes\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
.

Definition 4.2

(
[
VV10
]
)
.

The
Varagnolo–Vasserot algebra of quantum differential operators

\mathcal{D}
is the subalgebra
\mathcal{O}\rtimes\partial_{\triangleright}(\mathcal{O})
of the smash product
\mathcal{O}\rtimes\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
.

The Varagnolo–Vasserot algebra does indeed satisfy our desired equivariance.
Letting
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
act on itself via the adjoint action,
\mathcal{O}\rtimes\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
is a
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
-module-algebra.
Restricting this action to
\Delta(\mathcal{U})\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
, we obtain an action of
\mathcal{U}
on
\mathcal{D}
that gives the coadjoint action on
\mathcal{O}
and the adjoint action on
\partial_{\triangleright}(\mathcal{O})
.
We will also use
\bowtie
to denote this
\mathcal{U}
action on
\mathcal{D}
.

Finally, we note that by (
4.1
), commuting
\partial_{\triangleright}(\mathcal{O})
past
\mathcal{O}
in
\mathcal{D}
does not cleanly incorporate the left coregular action because the right tensorand of
\mathcal{O}
is also affected.
However, the discrepancy has a diagrammatic interpretation that is cleaner than the symbolic formula gotten by combining (
3.19
) and (
4.1
):

\partial_{\triangleright}(v^{*}\otimes v)(w^{*}\otimes w)=\big(\partial_{\triangleright}(v^{*}\otimes
