(4.4)

4.1.4.
Basic representation

The smash product
\mathcal{O}\rtimes\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
has an action on
\mathcal{O}
where
\mathcal{O}
acts by multiplication and
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
acts via the left and and right coregular actions.
This action is
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
-equivariant.
Moreover, from the definition of the smash product (
4.3
), we have that
\mathcal{O}
is an induced representation:

(4.5)

\mathcal{O}\cong\mathcal{O}\rtimes\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\bigg/\mathcal{O}\rtimes\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}-\epsilon(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2})\right)

We call the representation
\mathcal{O}
and its restriction to
\mathcal{D}
the
basic representation
.

\mathcal{O}
is then a
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant
\mathcal{D}
-module (using the
\bowtie
action).
Note that even though the smash product relations do not cleanly incorporate the left coregular action,
\partial_{\triangleright}(\mathcal{O})
does indeed act on
\mathcal{O}
via the left coregular action.
This follows from (
4.1
) and (
4.5
).

4.2.
Monodromy matrices

Recall the generating matrix
M
for
\mathcal{O}
given in
3.4.2
.
Let
\{a^{i}_{j}\}
denote a copy of
\{m^{i}_{j}\}
given by
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{D}
and let
\{b^{i}_{j}\}
denote another copy given by
\{\partial_{\triangleright}(m^{i}_{j})\}
.
We define the matrices
A
,
B
,
A^{-1}
, and
B^{-1}
by:

\displaystyle A_{j}^{i}

\displaystyle=E_{j}^{i}\otimes a_{j}^{i},

\displaystyle(A^{-1})^{i}_{j}

\displaystyle=E_{j}^{i}\otimes\iota(a^{i}_{j}),

\displaystyle B_{j}^{i}

\displaystyle=E_{j}^{i}\otimes b_{j}^{i},

\displaystyle(B^{-1})_{j}^{i}

\displaystyle=E_{j}^{i}\otimes\iota(b^{i}_{j}):=E_{j}^{i}\otimes\partial_{\triangleright}(\iota(m^{i}_{j}))

4.2.1.
Determinant bigrading

We set:

\displaystyle\textstyle\det_{q}(A)

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{qcoev}_{\mathbb{1}_{1}}(1)\otimes 1\in\mathcal{O}\otimes 1\subset\mathcal{D}

\displaystyle\textstyle\det_{q}(B)

\displaystyle:=\partial_{\triangleright}\mathrm{qcoev}_{\mathbb{1}_{1}}(1)\in 1\otimes\kappa(\mathcal{O})\subset\mathcal{D}

As in
3.4.2
, these elements can be written in terms of the entries of
A
and
B
, respectively.
Moreover,
\det_{q}(A)
commutes with
\mathcal{O}\otimes 1
and
\det_{q}(B)
commutes with
1\otimes\kappa(\mathcal{O})
.
Their commutation relations with elements from their respective “opposite” tensor factors are also nice.

First note that, from the factorization (
3.6
) and the formulas (
3.9
) for the determinant representation, we have:

(4.6)

Using these local relations on (
4.4
), one can see that
\det_{q}(B)
satisfies:

Similarly,
\det_{q}(A)
satisfies:

Thus, we can define an internal bigrading on
\mathcal{D}
:
x\in\mathcal{D}
is homogeneous of degree
(a,b)
if

\displaystyle\textstyle\det_{q}(B)x

\displaystyle=q^{2a}x\textstyle\det_{q}(B)

\displaystyle\textstyle\det_{q}(A)x

\displaystyle=q^{-2b}x\textstyle\det_{q}(A)

The entries of
A
and
B
have degrees
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
, respectively.

4.2.2.
Double R-matrix presentation

The following is Proposition 1.8.3(b) of
[
VV10
]
:

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{+}
be the algebra with generators given by the entries of
A
and
B
and relations

(4.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}R_{21}A_{13}R_{12}A_{23}&=A_{23}R_{21}A_{13}R_{12}\\
R_{21}B_{13}R_{12}B_{23}&=B_{23}R_{21}B_{13}R_{12}\\
R_{21}B_{13}R_{12}A_{23}&=A_{23}R_{12}B_{13}R_{21}^{-1}\end{split}

The elements
\{\det_{q}(A),\det_{q}(B)\}
generate an Ore set in
\mathcal{D}_{+}
and
\mathcal{D}
is isomorphic to its localization.

The novel third relation is a rewriting of (
4.4
), cf.
[
VV10
, A.5]
.
This algebra was defined prior to
[
VV10
]
by Alekseev and Schomerus
[
AS96
]
as an intermediate step to constructing their quantized character variety for the once-punctured torus.
There, the
A
- and
B
-matrices respectively quantize monodromy matrices along the
a
- and
b
-cycles of the torus.

4.3.
Quantum Weyl algebra

A reference for this section is
[
GZ95
]
.
The
quantum Weyl algebra
of rank
n
, denoted
\mathcal{W}
, is the
\mathbb{C}(q)
-algebra with generators

\left\{\xi_{i},\partial_{i}\,|\,1\leq i\leq n\right\}

and relations

(4.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}&=q\xi_{j}\xi_{i}\hbox{ for }i>j\\
\partial_{i}\partial_{j}&=q^{-1}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\hbox{ for }i>j\\
\partial_{i}\xi_{j}&=q\xi_{j}\partial_{i}\hbox{ for }i\not=j\\
\partial_{i}\xi_{i}&=1+q^{2}\xi_{i}\partial_{i}+(q^{2}-1)\sum_{j<i}\xi_{j}\partial_{j}\end{split}

If we set
\deg\xi_{i}=1
and
\deg\partial_{i}=-1
, then the relations (
4.8
) respect the grading.
We denote by
\mathcal{W}_{0}
the subalgebra of degree 0 elements.

Remark 4.4
.

In
[
Jor14
]
, there is a parameter
t
that the author then sets to
t=1
(cf. Remark 3.11 of
loc. cit.
).
Here, we instead set
t=q^{-1}
.
This affects the formula for the moment map later in
4.4.2
.

4.3.1.
Equivariance

\mathcal{W}
has an RTT-type presentation (cf.
[
Jor14
, Example 4.9]
): if we define the vectors

\displaystyle\vec{\xi}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{i}\otimes\xi_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}&\xi_{2}&\cdots&\xi_{n}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\vec{\partial}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\otimes\partial_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}\\
\partial_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\partial_{n}\end{pmatrix}

in
\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathcal{W}
then relations (
4.8
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle q\vec{\xi}_{13}\vec{\xi}_{23}

\displaystyle=\vec{\xi}_{23}\vec{\xi}_{13}R

\displaystyle q\vec{\partial}_{13}\vec{\partial}_{23}

\displaystyle=R\vec{\partial}_{23}\vec{\partial}_{13}

\displaystyle q^{-1}\vec{\partial}_{23}\vec{\xi}_{13}

\displaystyle=\vec{\xi}_{13}R\vec{\partial}_{23}+q^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{i}\otimes e_{i}\otimes 1

This algebra can be written as a quotient of the tensor algebra
\mathcal{T}(\mathbb{V}\oplus\mathbb{V}^{*})
by images of the braidings and evaluations.
Here,
\partial_{i}
corresponds to
e^{i}\in\mathbb{V}^{*}
and
\xi_{i}
corresponds to
e_{i}\in\mathbb{V}
.
Consequently, we have:

Proposition 4.5
.

\mathcal{W}
is a
\mathcal{U}
-module-algebra.

We denote the
\mathcal{U}
-action on
\mathcal{W}
by
\bullet
.
Note that the degree is given by the action of
q^{\omega_{n}}
.
Since
q^{\omega_{n}}
is central in
\mathcal{U}
, it follows that
\mathcal{W}_{0}
is a
\mathcal{U}
-submodule.

4.3.2.
Functional representation

Let
\mathcal{W}_{\xi}
be the subalgebra generated by
\left\{\xi_{i}\right\}
.
As a
\mathcal{U}
-module, the subalgebra
\mathcal{W}_{\xi}
is isomorphic to the
quantum symmetric algebra
of
\mathbb{V}
.

S_{q}\mathbb{V}:=\bigoplus_{m=0}^{\infty}S_{q}^{m}\mathbb{V}

The ordered monomials

\xi_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots\xi_{n-1}^{k_{n-1}}\xi_{n}^{k_{n}}

form a basis of
S_{q}\mathbb{V}
(cf. Theorem
5.1
below).
We can extend the natural multiplication action of
\mathcal{W}_{\xi}
to one of the entirety of
\mathcal{W}
by setting

\partial_{i}(\xi_{1}^{k_{1}}\xi_{2}^{k_{2}}\cdots\xi_{i}^{k_{i}}\cdots\xi_{n}^{k_{n}})=(q\xi_{1})^{k_{1}}(q\xi_{2})^{k_{2}}\cdots[k_{i}]_{q^{2}}\xi_{i}^{k_{i}-1}\xi_{i+1}^{k_{i+1}}\cdots\xi_{n}^{k_{n}}

This action is merely the one induced by quotienting
\mathcal{T}(\mathbb{V}\oplus\mathbb{V}^{*})
by the left ideal generated by
\mathbb{V}^{*}
.
As such, this action is
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant.
The action of
\mathcal{W}_{0}
on
S_{q}\mathbb{V}
preserves each piece
S_{q}^{m}\mathbb{V}
.

4.3.3.
Difference operators

It will be useful to interpret the functional representation in terms of difference operators in commuting variables.
Let
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathbf{z}_{n}]:=\mathbb{C}(q)[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]
and let
T_{q,z_{i}}
denote the
q
-shift operator:

T_{q,z_{i}}z_{j}=q^{\delta_{i,j}}z_{j}

For an integer vector
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, we define

\displaystyle z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=z_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots z_{n}^{\lambda_{n}},

\displaystyle T_{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=T_{q,z_{1}}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots T_{q,z_{n}}^{\lambda_{n}}

Consider the following rings of difference operators:

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{q}(\mathbf{z}_{n})

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}a_{\lambda,\mu}z^{\lambda}T_{\mu}\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}a_{\lambda,\mu}\in\mathbb{C}(q)\\
\hbox{and only finitely many }a_{\lambda,\mu}\not=0\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{q}^{+}(\mathbf{z}_{n})

\displaystyle=\left\{D\in\mathbb{D}_{q}(\mathbf{z}_{n})\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}Df\in\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathbf{z}_{n}]\\
\hbox{for all }f\in\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathbf{z}_{n}]\end{array}\right\}

Proposition 4.6
.

\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathbf{z}_{n}]
is a faithful representation of
\mathbb{D}_{q}^{+}(\mathbf{z}_{n})
.

The map

\xi_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots\xi_{n}^{k_{n}}\mapsto z_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots z_{n}^{k_{n}}

induces a vector space isomorphism
S_{q}\mathbb{V}\cong\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathbf{z}_{n}]
.
Carrying over the actions of
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{W}
, we obtain homomorphisms of both algebras into
\mathbb{D}_{q}^{+}(\mathbf{z}_{n})
, both of which we denote by
\mathfrak{qdiff}
:

(4.9)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{qdiff}(q^{\epsilon_{i}})

\displaystyle=T_{q,z_{i}}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{qdiff}(\xi_{i})

\displaystyle=z_{i}T_{q,z_{1}}\cdots T_{q,z_{i-1}}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{qdiff}(E_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{z_{i}}{z_{i+1}}\left(\frac{T_{q,z_{i+1}}-T_{q,z_{i+1}}^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}\right)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{qdiff}(\partial_{i})

\displaystyle=z_{i}^{-1}T_{q,z_{1}}\cdots T_{q,z_{i-1}}\left(\frac{T_{q,z_{i}}^{2}-1}{q^{2}-1}\right)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{qdiff}(F_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{z_{i+1}}{z_{i}}\left(\frac{T_{q,z_{i}}-T_{q,z_{i}}^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}\right)

Since
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathbf{z}_{n}]\cong S_{q}\mathbb{V}
is a faithful
\mathbb{D}^{+}_{q}(\mathbf{z}_{n})
-module, the fact that
S_{q}\mathbb{V}
is a
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant
\mathcal{W}
-module implies that
\mathfrak{qdiff}
pieces together into an algebra homomorphism out of the smash product:

\mathfrak{qdiff}:\mathcal{W}\rtimes\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}^{+}_{q}(\mathbf{z}_{n})

4.4.
Quantum Hamiltonian reduction

To perform quantum Hamiltonian reduction, we will need a notion of quantum moment maps in our Hopf-algebraic setting.
Let
H
be a Hopf algebra acting on an algebra
A
such that
A
is an
H
-module-algebra.
If we denote this action by

(-)\blacktriangleright(-):H\otimes A\rightarrow A

then a
quantum moment map
(in the sense of
[
VV10
]
) for the action is an algebra homomorphism
\mu:H\rightarrow A
such that for
h\in H
and
a\in A
,

(4.10)

\mu(h)a=(h_{(1)}\blacktriangleright a)\mu(h_{(2)})

More generally, we can define quantum moment maps for the action of a left coideal subalgebra
H^{\prime}\subset H
,
In our case, we will be working with
H=\mathcal{U}
and
H^{\prime}=\kappa(\mathcal{O})
.

4.4.1.
Moment map for
\mathcal{D}

The following is Proposition 1.8.3(a) of
[
VV10
]
and Proposition 7.21 of
[
Jor14
]
:

Proposition 4.7
.

The map
\mu_{\mathcal{D}}:\kappa(\mathcal{O})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
given by

(4.11)

(1\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{D}}\circ\kappa)(M)=BA^{-1}B^{-1}A

is a quantum moment map for the
\bowtie
-action restricted to the left coideal subalgebra
\kappa(\mathcal{O})\subset\mathcal{U}
.
Moreover, it is
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant.

We emphasize that we are viewing
\kappa(\mathcal{O})
as a left coideal subalgebra of
\mathcal{U}
.
Thus, in (
4.10
), we are are taking the coproduct
\Delta
instead of
\nabla
.

Proposition 4.8
.

For
f\in\mathcal{O}
viewed as an element of the basic representation, we have

\mu_{\mathcal{D}}(x)f=\kappa(x)\bowtie f

Proof.

This was shown in the proof of Proposition 1.8.2(c) of
[
VV10
]
, but we give a diagrammatic proof.
It suffices to consider entries of the generating matrix
M
.
Using (
3.17
) and (
4.4
), we calculate the entries of
BA^{-1}B^{-1}A
images of the following a morphism, working right to left:

(4.12)

Acting on
V^{*}\otimes V\subset\mathcal{O}
, we obtain

upon applying the coend relation (
3.14
).
∎

4.4.2.
Moment map for
\mathcal{W}

The following was proved by Jordan
[
Jor14
]
:

Proposition 4.9
.

The action of the left coideal subalgebra
\kappa(\mathfrak{R})\subset\mathcal{U}
on
\mathcal{W}
has a quantum moment map

\mu_{\mathcal{W}}(m^{i}_{j})=\delta_{i,j}+(1-q^{-2})\partial_{i}\xi_{j}

This map is
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant.
The powers of
\mu_{\mathcal{W}}(\det_{q}(M))
form an Ore set.
Let
\mathcal{W}^{\circ}
denote the localization at those powers.
Then
\mu_{\mathcal{W}}
extends to a quantum moment map for
\kappa(\mathcal{O})
into
\mathcal{W}^{\circ}
.

Note that the image of
\mu_{\mathcal{W}}
lies in the degree zero part
\mathcal{W}^{\circ}_{0}
.

.

We have for
f\in S_{q}\mathbb{V}
,

\mu_{\mathcal{W}}(m^{i}_{j})f=\kappa(m^{i}_{j})\bullet f

We view
S_{q}\mathbb{V}
as the quotient of
\mathcal{W}
by the left ideal
I_{\partial}

\left\{\partial_{i}\right\}
.

, we have that after quotienting by

,

\mu_{\mathcal{W}}(m^{k}_{j})+I_{\partial}=\delta_{k,j}+I_{\partial}

The proposition follows once we note that

Corollary 4.11
.

Recall the map
\mathfrak{qdiff}
(
4.9
).
We have:

\mathfrak{qdiff}\circ\kappa=\mathfrak{qdiff}\circ\mu_{\mathcal{W}}

Corollary 4.12
.

The functional representation
S_{q}\mathbb{V}
is preserved under the action of the localization
\mathcal{W}^{\circ}
.

4.4.3.
Reduction

Finally, we consider the tensor product algebra

\mathcal{M}:=\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{W}^{\circ}

in the braided monoidal category of locally-finite
\mathcal{U}
-modules.
Thus, the product involves
\mathcal{R}
: for
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{D}
and
b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathcal{W}^{\circ}
,

(a_{1}\otimes b_{1})(a_{2}\otimes b_{2})=a_{1}(\mathchoice{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}\bowtie a_{2})\otimes(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-11.38931pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.92825pt}}_{{\kern-6.33623pt{s}\kern 3.92825pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-11.38931pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.92825pt}}_{{\kern-6.33623pt{s}\kern 3.92825pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-7.70789pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.34679pt}}_{{\kern-4.09853pt{s}\kern 2.34679pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-7.03737pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.67627pt}}_{{\kern-3.42801pt{s}\kern 1.67627pt}}}\bullet b_{1})b_{2}

With this braiding,
\mathcal{M}
is a
\mathcal{U}
-module algebra and thus
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{U}}
is an algebra.

Next, we collate the moment maps into one for
\mathcal{M}
.
We define

\mu_{\mathcal{M}}:=(\mu_{\mathcal{D}}\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{W}})\circ(\kappa\otimes\kappa)\circ\nabla:\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}

It is a
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant algebra homomorphism (each of its components are).
The following is Proposition 3.10 of
[
GJS19
]
, which features a nice diagrammatic proof:

Proposition 4.13
.

The map
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
is a quantum moment map.

Recall the determinant character
\chi^{k}
defined in (
3.9
).
We will abuse notation and use
\chi^{k}
to also denote the induced character
\chi^{k}\circ\kappa
of
\mathcal{O}
.
In terms of the generating matrix
M
of
\mathcal{O}
, we can use the factorization of
\mathcal{R}
(
3.6
) to deduce

(1\otimes\chi^{k})(M)=q^{-2k}I

Definition 4.14
.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{k}\subset\mathcal{M}
be the following left ideal:

\mathcal{I}_{k}:=\mathcal{M}\left((1\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{M}})(M)-q^{-2(k-1)}I\right)

The
quantized multiplicative quiver variety
is defined to be
\mathcal{A}_{k}:=\big(\mathcal{M}\big/\mathcal{I}_{k}\big)^{\mathcal{U}}
.

Proposition 4.15

(
[
VV10
,
GJS19
]
)
.

\mathcal{A}_{k}
is an algebra.

4.4.4.
Radial parts map

Denote by

\left(\mathcal{O}\otimes S_{q}\mathbb{V}\right)^{\chi^{k}}

the subspace where
\mathcal{U}
acts by the character
\chi^{k}
.
From the Peter-Weyl decomposition for
\mathcal{O}
(
3.15
), we can see that
q^{\omega_{n}}
acts trivally on
\mathcal{O}
.
Thus,

\left(\mathcal{O}\otimes S_{q}\mathbb{V}\right)^{\chi_{k}}=\left(\mathcal{O}\otimes S_{q}^{nk}\mathbb{V}\right)^{\chi^{k}}

We can further identify

\left(\mathcal{O}\otimes S_{q}^{nk}\mathbb{V}\right)^{\chi^{k}}\cong(\mathcal{O}\otimes U_{k})^{\mathcal{U}}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P^{+}}\left(V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda}\otimes U_{k}\right)^{\mathcal{U}}

By Theorem
3.11
, we can identify the last space with
\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(q)
by taking the weighted trace.

\mathcal{M}
acts on the tensor product representation
\mathcal{O}\otimes S_{q}\mathbb{V}
, where this tensor product representation is taken in the braided monoidal category of locally-finite
\mathcal{U}
-modules.
Therefore, the action involves the braiding between
\mathcal{W}^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{O}
and the action map is
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant.
This action on
\mathcal{O}\otimes S_{q}\mathbb{V}
then restricts to an action of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{U}}
on
\left(\mathcal{O}\otimes S_{q}\mathbb{V}\right)^{\chi^{k}}
.

Proposition 4.16
.

For
k\geq 0
, the action of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{U}}
on
\left(\mathcal{O}\otimes S_{q}\mathbb{V}\right)^{\chi^{k}}
factors to an action of
\mathcal{A}_{k+1}
.

Proof.

We first show that
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(m^{i}_{j})
acts on
\mathcal{O}\otimes S_{q}\mathbb{V}
as
\kappa(m^{i}_{j})
(i.e. via the
\mathcal{U}
-action).
Let
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{M}}
be the action map of
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathcal{O}\otimes S_{q}\mathbb{V}
.
Using Propositions
4.8
and
4.10
, we have:

It follows that the restricted action map

\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{M}\otimes\left(\mathcal{O}\otimes S_{q}\mathbb{V}\right)^{\chi^{k}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}\otimes S_{q}\mathbb{V}

factors through
\mathcal{I}_{k+1}
.
The result follows from taking invariants of
\mathcal{M}\big/\mathcal{I}_{k+1}
.
∎

Definition 4.17
.

We denote the representation map by
\mathfrak{rad}_{k}:\mathcal{A}_{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}\left(\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(q)\right)
and called it the
quantum radial parts map
.

Proposition 4.18
.

For
k>n
, the image of
\mathfrak{rad}_{k}
contains the image of
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})
under
\mathfrak{r}
:

\mathfrak{rad}_{k}\left(\mathcal{A}_{k}\right)\supset\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})\right)

Moreover, this inclusion respects the bigradings.

Proof.

Recall the generators of
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})
from Lemma
2.5
(in the case
k>n
).
Our analysis in
3.5.3
shows that

(4.13)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{rad}_{k}\left(\mathrm{qcoev}_{\wedge_{q}^{r}\mathbb{V}}(1)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathbf{s}e_{r}(\mathbf{X}_{n})\mathbf{s}\right)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{rad}_{k}\left(\partial_{\triangleright}\mathrm{qcoev}_{\wedge_{q}^{r}\mathbb{V}^{*}}(1)\right)

\displaystyle=q^{r(n-1)}\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathbf{s}e_{r}(\mathbf{Y}^{-1}_{n})\mathbf{s}\right)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{rad}_{k}\left(\textstyle\det_{q}(A)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathbf{s}\mathbf{X}^{-1}_{n}\mathbf{s}\right)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{rad}_{k}\left(\textstyle\det_{q}(B)\right)

\displaystyle=q^{-n(n-1)}\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathbf{s}\mathbf{Y}_{n}\mathbf{s}\right)

The last two imply the statement about bigradings.
∎

5.
Isomorphism

5.1.
Classical degeneration

Recall that
\mathcal{D}_{+}\subset\mathcal{D}
is the subalgebra generated by the entries of
A
and
B
(so we exclude those of
A^{-1}
and
B^{-1}
).
From the relations (
4.7
) and the formula (
3.7
) for
R
, we can expect that
\mathcal{D}_{+}
becomes the commutative ring
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n}]
when
q\mapsto 1
.
Similarly, the quantum Weyl algebra
\mathcal{W}
(
4.8
) should degenerate to the usual Weyl algebra
D(\mathbb{C}^{n})
.
Here, we review work of Jordan
[
Jor14
]
that makes this precise and we enhance these results to address invariants.

5.1.1.
Lattices

Let

\mathcal{M}_{+}:=\mathcal{D}_{+}\otimes\mathcal{W}

(as in
4.4.3
, we mean the
braided
tensor product of algebras).
As in
[
Jor14
]
, we define a
standard monomial
in
\mathcal{M}_{+}
to be a product

(5.1)

a^{i_{1}}_{j_{1}}\cdots a^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}b^{k_{1}}_{\ell_{1}}\cdots b^{k_{\beta}}_{\ell_{\beta}}\xi_{r_{1}}\cdots\xi_{r_{\gamma}}\partial_{s_{1}}\cdots\partial_{s_{\delta}}

where if
u<v
,

(5.2)

\displaystyle i_{u}<i_{v}\hbox{ or }\left(i_{u}=i_{v}\hbox{ and }j_{u}\leq j_{v}\right),

\displaystyle k_{u}<k_{v}\hbox{ or }\left(\ell_{r}=\ell_{s}\hbox{ and }j_{u}\leq j_{v}\right)

\displaystyle r_{u}\leq r_{v},

\displaystyle s_{u}\leq s_{v}

whenever such indices are present.

Theorem 5.1

(
[
Jor14
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{+,\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}
be the
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]
-subalgebra generated by the generators
\{a^{i}_{j},b^{k}_{\ell},\xi_{r},\partial_{s}\}
.
The standard monomials form a basis of
\mathcal{M}_{+,\mathbb{Z}}
.
Thus,

\left[\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]\bigg/(q-1)\right]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]}\mathcal{M}_{+,\mathbb{Z}}\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n}]\otimes D(\mathbb{C}^{n})

where
D(\mathbb{C}^{n})
is the Weyl algebra.

For technical reasons, we will need a slight alteration of this result.
First, we consider instead the subalgebra
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{IV}}\subset\mathcal{D}
generated by the entries of
A
and
B^{-1}
and correspondingly set

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}:=\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{IV}}\otimes\mathcal{W}

Next, we will replace
\partial_{i}
with

\widetilde{\partial}_{i}:=(1-q^{-2})\partial_{i}

We then define a standard monomial to be

a^{i_{1}}_{j_{1}}\cdots a^{i_{\alpha}}_{j_{\alpha}}\iota(b^{k_{\beta}}_{\ell_{\beta}})\cdots\iota(b^{k_{1}}_{\ell_{1}})\xi_{r_{1}}\cdots\xi_{r_{\gamma}}\widetilde{\partial}_{s_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{\partial}_{s_{\delta}}

where the indices still satisfy (
5.2
) when
u<v
.

Let

\mathfrak{M}:=\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathfrak{gl_{n}}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\left(\mathbb{C}^{n}\right)^{*}

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV},\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}
be the
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]
-subalgebra generated by
\{a^{i}_{j},\iota(b^{i}_{j}),\xi_{r},\widetilde{\partial}_{s}\}
.
The standard monomials form a basis of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV,\mathbb{Z}}}
.
Moreover,

\left[\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]\bigg/(q-1)\right]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV},\mathbb{Z}}\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}]

Proof.

The basis statement is clear because
\iota
is an algebra anti-automorphism—we can map
\mathcal{M}_{+}
isomorphically to
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}
within
\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{W}
in a manner that sends standard monomials to standard monomials.
Finally, note that from (
4.8
), we have:

\widetilde{\partial}_{i}\xi_{i}=(1-q^{-2})+q^{2}\xi_{i}\widetilde{\partial}_{i}+(q^{2}-1)\sum_{k>i}\xi_{k}\widetilde{\partial}_{k}

Therefore,
\left[\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]\big/(q-1)\right]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV},\mathbb{Z}}
is a commutative ring.
∎

Thus, whenever we perform
q\mapsto 1
degeneration in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}
, it will always be with respect to this basis of standard monomials.
Namely, for any subspace
V\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}
, we define:

(5.3)

\displaystyle V_{\mathbb{Z}}

\displaystyle:=V\cap\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV},\mathbb{Z}}

\displaystyle V_{q=1}

\displaystyle:=\left\{f_{1}(1)M_{1}+\cdots+f_{m}(1)M_{m}\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}f_{1}(q)M_{1}+\cdots+f_{m}(q)M_{m}\in V_{\mathbb{Z}}\\
\hbox{and }M_{1},\ldots,M_{m}\hbox{ are standard monomials}\end{array}\right\}

Proposition 5.3
.

For a finite-dimensional subspace
V\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}
, we have:

(5.6)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}(q)}V=\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]}V_{\mathbb{Z}}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V_{q=1}

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV,\mathbb{Z}}}
is free and
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]
is a PID.
∎

5.1.2.
Invariants

We endow
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}]=\mathbb{C}\left[\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\left(\mathbb{C}^{n}\right)^{*}\right]
with the following
GL_{n}
-action:
for the natural coordinate functions
(X,Y,i,j)
on
\mathfrak{M}
and
g\in GL_{n}
, let

g(X,Y,i,j)=(g^{-1}Xg,g^{-1}Yg,(g^{-1})i,jg)

Here, we view the last factor
j
as a row vector.

U(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
then acts on
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}]
via derivations induced by the coadjoint and vector representations.
By a classic theorem of Weyl
[
Wey39
]
,
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}]^{GL_{n}}
is generated by
classical trace functions
:

(5.7)

\mathrm{tr}(X^{a_{1}}Y^{b_{1}}(ij)^{c_{1}}\cdots X^{a_{m}}Y^{b_{m}}(ij)^{c_{m}})

for
a_{1},b_{1},c_{1},a_{2},b_{2},c_{2},\ldots,a_{m},b_{m},c_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

We identify
(A,B^{-1},(1-q^{-2})\vec{\partial},\vec{\xi})
with
(X,Y,i,j)
in the
q\mapsto 1
degeneration as follows:

(5.8)

A\mapsto X,B^{-1}\mapsto Y,(1-q^{-2})\vec{\partial}\mapsto i,\vec{\xi}\mapsto j

With this, let us define natural quantum versions of (
5.7
).
For
k\geq 0
, we define the
quantum trace
of
M^{k}
as:

(5.9)

\mathrm{tr}_{q}(M^{k}):=\sum_{1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\leq n}q^{-\langle 2\rho,\epsilon_{i_{k}}\rangle}m^{i_{k}}_{i_{1}}m^{i_{1}}_{i_{2}}\cdots m^{i_{k-1}}_{i_{k}}

Similarly, for
a_{1},b_{1},c_{1},a_{2},b_{2},c_{2},\ldots,a_{m},b_{m},c_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we define

(5.10)

\mathrm{tr}_{q}\left(A^{a_{1}}B^{-b_{1}}(\mu_{\mathcal{W}}(M)-I)^{c_{1}}\cdots A^{a_{m}}B^{-b_{m}}(\mu_{\mathcal{W}}(M)-I)^{c_{m}}\right)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}

as (
5.9
) with
m_{j}^{i}
replaced with
a^{i}_{j}
,
\iota(b)^{i}_{j}
, or

\left(\mu_{\mathcal{W}}(M)-I\right)^{i}_{j}=\widetilde{\partial}_{i}\xi_{j}

depending on the location.
We call these
quantum trace elements
.

Proposition 5.4
.

The quantum traces are elements of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}^{\mathcal{U}}\cap\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV,\mathbb{Z}}}
that are sent to their corresponding classical traces when
q\mapsto 1
.

Proof.

It is obvious that quantum traces are contained in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV},\mathbb{Z}}
and are sent to (
5.7
) when
q\mapsto 1
.
To see that they are
\mathcal{U}
-invariants, observe that they are the image of
1\in\mathbb{1}
under a composition of
\mathcal{U}
-morphisms:

(1)

the map
\mathbb{1}\rightarrow(\mathbb{V}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{V})^{\otimes k}
induced by

1\mapsto\sum_{1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\leq n}q^{-\langle 2\rho,\epsilon_{i_{k}}\rangle}m^{i_{k}}_{i_{1}}\otimes m^{i_{1}}_{i_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes m^{i_{k-1}}_{i_{k}}

which is depicted diagrammatically as

(2)

the antipode
\iota
on tensorands that will correspond to
B^{-1}
matrix elements;

(3)

the inclusion into
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}
(identity for
A
-matrix elements,
\partial_{\triangleright}
for
B^{-1}
-matrix elements, and
\mu_{W}-\mathrm{ev}_{\mathbb{V}}
);

(4)

the product in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}
.∎

Lemma 5.5
.

For any
\mathcal{U}
-submodule
V\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}
,
V_{q=1}
is a
GL_{n}
-module.
Furthermore, we have

\left[V^{\mathcal{U}}\right]_{q=1}=\left[V_{q=1}\right]^{GL_{n}}

Proof.

Observe that
\mathcal{U}
acts on standard monomials in a way that preserves the lattice
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV},\mathbb{Z}}
.
Moreover, because of (
5.8
), the action of
E_{i}
and
F_{i}
degenerate to the action of their corresponding generators
e_{i},f_{i}\in U(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
on monomials in
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}]
.
Finally, the degeneration preserves the weight.
Thus,
V_{q=1}
is a
GL_{n}
-module and we have the containment

\left[V^{\mathcal{U}}\right]_{q=1}\subset\left[V_{q=1}\right]^{GL_{n}}

For the other containment, let
v\in V_{\mathbb{Z}}
be a lift of
\mathfrak{v}\in\left[V_{q=1}\right]^{GL_{n}}
.
We can write
\mathfrak{v}
in terms of classical traces.
Let
\mathrm{tr}_{q}^{\mathfrak{v}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV},\mathbb{Z}}
be the element where every classical trace is replaced with its quantum version.
We can write

v=\mathrm{tr}_{q}^{\mathfrak{v}}+(q-1)w

for some
w\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV,\mathbb{Z}}}
.
Since
\mathrm{tr}_{q}^{\mathfrak{v}}
is
\mathcal{U}
-invariant, we have that

\displaystyle E_{i}v

\displaystyle=(q-1)E_{i}w,

\displaystyle F_{i}v

\displaystyle=(q-1)F_{i}w

for all
i
.
If
v\not\in V^{\mathcal{U}}
, then one of the expressions above is nonzero and
w\in V
due to complete reducibility (
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}
is locally-finite).
It then follows that
\mathrm{tr}_{q}^{\mathfrak{v}}
is an element of
V^{\mathcal{U}}\cap\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV},\mathbb{Z}}
that lifts
\mathfrak{v}
.
∎

We can combine (
5.6
) with Lemma
5.5
to obtain:

Proposition 5.6
.

For a finite-dimensional
\mathcal{U}
-submodule
V\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}
,

(5.11)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}(q)}V^{\mathcal{U}}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left[V_{q=1}\right]^{GL_{n}}

5.2.
The case
\mathbf{t=q^{k}}

Proposition
4.18
shows that
\mathcal{A}_{k}
is at least as large as
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})
.
Our strategy to handle the
t=q^{k}
case is to show that
\mathcal{A}_{k}
is also no bigger than
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})
.
Of course, this is but an impressionistic statement—we will need to make it precise.

5.2.1.
Mise en place

We begin with a little result that allows us to focus on
\mathcal{D}
:

Lemma 5.7
.

The image of the composition

\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{U}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{U}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{k}

becomes all of
\mathcal{A}_{k}
after performing a localization.

Proof.

First note that by considering the action of
q^{\omega_{n}}
, any
\mathcal{U}
-invariant of
\mathcal{M}
must have degree zero in its
\mathcal{W}
component, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\circ}\right)^{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}\cap\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\circ}\right)^{\mathcal{U}}

From multiplication
on the left
, we have

(5.12)

\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{0}\bigg(\delta_{i,j}+(q-q^{-1})\partial_{i}\xi_{j}-q^{-2k}\sum_{i}(A^{-1}BAB^{-1})_{j}^{i}\bigg)\subset\mathcal{I}_{k}\cap\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{0}

On the other hand,
\mathcal{W}_{0}
is generated by
\{\partial_{i}\xi_{j}\}
.
For any
w\in\mathcal{W}_{0}
and
x\in\mathcal{D}
, we can use the R-matrix to move
w
to the right:

wx=(\mathchoice{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}\bowtie x)(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-11.38931pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.92825pt}}_{{\kern-6.33623pt{s}\kern 3.92825pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-11.38931pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.92825pt}}_{{\kern-6.33623pt{s}\kern 3.92825pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-7.70789pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.34679pt}}_{{\kern-4.09853pt{s}\kern 2.34679pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-7.03737pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.67627pt}}_{{\kern-3.42801pt{s}\kern 1.67627pt}}}\bullet w)

We can then use (
5.12
) to write
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-11.38931pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.92825pt}}_{{\kern-6.33623pt{s}\kern 3.92825pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-11.38931pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.92825pt}}_{{\kern-6.33623pt{s}\kern 3.92825pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-7.70789pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.34679pt}}_{{\kern-4.09853pt{s}\kern 2.34679pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-7.03737pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.67627pt}}_{{\kern-3.42801pt{s}\kern 1.67627pt}}}\bullet w
in terms of
\mathcal{D}
in the quotient
\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{0}\big/(\mathcal{I}_{k}\cap\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{0})
.
Therefore, the map

\mathcal{D}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{0}\big/(\mathcal{I}_{k}\cap\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{0})

is surjective.
Since all the
\mathcal{U}
-modules involved are locally-finite, taking
\mathcal{U}
-invariants is exact.
After doing so,
\det_{q}(M)
can be written in terms of elements of
\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{U}}
.
We localize the image at that latter expression.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

\mathcal{A}_{k}
is a localization of
\mathcal{A}_{k,\mathrm{IV}}:=\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{IV}}^{\mathcal{U}}\big/(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{IV}}\cap\mathcal{I}_{k})^{\mathcal{U}}
.

We can thus focus on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{IV}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{k,\mathrm{IV}}
.
The left ideal
\mathcal{I}_{k}
is generated by elements with homogeneous determinant bigrading, and so
\mathcal{A}_{k}
and
\mathcal{A}_{k,\mathrm{IV}}
are also bigraded.
Let
\mathcal{A}_{k,\mathrm{IV}}[a,b]
denote its bidegree
(a,b)
piece.
Recall our notation from
2.2.1
.
Proposition
4.18
implies that

(5.13)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathcal{A}_{k,\mathrm{IV}}[a,-b]\geq\dim_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathcal{S}_{q^{k},\mathrm{IV}}[a,-b]=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}\left[\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n}\right]^{\Sigma_{n}}_{a,b}

We will use classical degeneration, as covered in
5.1
, to obtain the opposite bound.

5.2.2.
Almost commuting variety

Let
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{ac}}\subset\mathfrak{M}=\mathbb{C}\left[\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\left(\mathbb{C}^{n}\right)^{*}\right]
be the closed subscheme with ideal generated by the entries of

(5.14)

[X,Y]-ij

where
(X,Y,i,j)
are the usual coordinates of
\mathfrak{M}
.
The following results are proved by Gan–Ginzburg
[
GG06
]
:

Theorem 5.9
.

The projection map
p:\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathfrak{gl_{n}}
induces an isomorphism:

(5.15)

\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{ac}}]^{GL_{n}}\cong\left(\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n}]\bigg/I\right)^{GL_{n}}\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n}]^{\Sigma_{n}}

where
I
is the ideal generated by the equations
[X,Y]=0
and the second isomorphism is induced by restriction to diagonal matrices.

\mathfrak{M}_{\textrm{ac}}
is called the
almost commuting variety
.

We can endow
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}]
with a bigrading where the entries of
X
have bidegree
(1,0)
and those of
Y
have bidegree
(0,1)
.
The ideal (
5.14
) identifies elements of bidegree
(1,1)
with those of bidegree
(0,0)
, so
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{ac}}]
inherits this bigrading.
Let
\mathbb{C}\langle X,Y\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{ac}}]
denote the subalgebra generated by the entries of
X
and
Y
and let
\mathbb{C}\langle X,Y\rangle^{GL_{n}}_{a,b}
denote the bidegree
(a,b)
piece of the invariant subalgebra.
Tracing through the isomorphism (
5.15
), we have:

Corollary 5.10
.

For each bidegree
(a,b)
,

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}\langle X,Y\rangle_{a,b}^{GL_{n}}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n}]^{\Sigma_{n}}_{a,b}

Lemma 5.11
.

There is an algebra homomorphism

\psi:\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{ac}}]\rightarrow\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}\right)_{q=1}\bigg/\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}\cap\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}\right)_{q=1}

that restricts to a surjection

\psi:\mathbb{C}\langle X,Y\rangle\twoheadrightarrow\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{IV}}\right)_{q=1}\bigg/\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}\cap\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{IV}}\right)_{q=1}

which respects bigradings.

Proof.

From
left
multiplication on the generators of
\mathcal{I}_{k}
, we can see that

(5.16)

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}\bigg((B^{-1}A)^{i}_{j}+(B^{-1}A)^{i}_{\ell}\widetilde{\partial}_{\ell}\xi_{j}-q^{-2k}(AB^{-1})^{i}_{j}\bigg)\subset\mathcal{I}_{k}\cap\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}

Let
(A,B^{-1},\vec{\xi},\vec{\partial})
denote the coordinates of
\mathrm{Spec}\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}\right)_{q=1}\cong\mathfrak{M}
and
(X,Y,i,j)
denote the coordinates for another copy of
\mathfrak{M}
.
Upon setting
q=1
, the containments (
5.16
) imply that

\left(X,Y,i,j\right)\mapsto(A,B^{-1},B^{-1}A\vec{\partial},\vec{\xi},)

induces the desired homomorphism
\psi
.
∎

Theorem 5.12
.

For
k>n
, the radial parts map
\mathfrak{rad}_{k}
gives an isomorphism

\mathcal{A}_{k}\cong\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})

Proof.

By Lemma
2.5
, equations (
4.13
), and Corollary
5.8
, it suffices to show that
\mathfrak{rad}_{k}
restricts to an isomorphism

\mathcal{A}_{k,\mathrm{IV}}\cong\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})_{\mathrm{IV}}

because localization is exact.
To that end, we just need to provide the opposite bound to (
5.13
).
Applying Propositions
5.3
and
5.6
and Lemma
5.5
, we have

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathcal{A}_{k,\mathrm{IV}}[a,b]

\displaystyle=\dim_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{IV}}^{\mathcal{U}}[a,b]-\dim_{\mathbb{C}(q)}\mathcal{I}_{k}\cap\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{IV}}^{\mathcal{U}}[a,b]

\displaystyle=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{IV}}[a,b]_{q=1}\right)^{GL_{n}}-\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}\cap\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{IV}}[a,b]_{q=1}\right)^{GL_{n}}

(5.17)

\displaystyle=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left[\left(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{IV}}\right)_{q=1}\bigg/\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}\cap\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{IV}}\right)_{q=1}\right]^{GL_{n}}_{a,b}

where in the final line, the subscript denotes the bidegree
(a,b)
piece.
Finally, we can combine Corollary
5.10
and Lemma
5.11
to bound (
5.17
) above by
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n}]_{a,b}^{\Sigma_{n}}
.
∎

5.3.
Generic parameters

Now we introduce the variable
t
and establish the isomorphism over
K=\mathbb{C}(q,t)
.
Let
\mathcal{U}_{t}:=K\otimes\mathcal{U}
.
We will base change
\mathcal{U}
and all its modules to
\mathcal{U}_{t}
but still denote them by the same symbols to avoid notational clutter.
Let
\alpha
be a parameter such that
t=q^{\alpha}
; we introduce it for stylistic/notational reasons so as to neatly replace the integer
k
.
All constructions below can be described fully in terms of
t
rather than
\alpha
.

5.3.1.
Etingof–Kirillov theory at
t

In order to extend
3.5
, we need a suitable generalization of
S_{q}^{nk}\mathbb{V}
, and moreover it should be a representation of
\mathcal{W}^{\circ}_{0}
.
Recall the notation from
4.3.3
, wherein we translated the actions of
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{W}
on
S_{q}\mathbb{V}
to ones on the polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}(q)[\mathbf{z}_{n}]:=\mathbb{C}(q)[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]
.
From this we constructed an algebra homomorphism

\mathfrak{qdiff}:\mathcal{W}\rtimes\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}_{q}(\mathbf{z}_{n})

where
\mathbb{D}_{q}(\mathbf{z}_{n})
is a ring of difference operators.
By Corollary
4.11
and the
\mathcal{U}
-equivariance of
\mu_{\mathcal{W}}
, we can extend
\mathfrak{qdiff}
to
\mathcal{W}^{\circ}\rtimes\mathcal{U}
by setting

\mathfrak{qdiff}(\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(\textstyle\det_{q}(M))^{-1})=(\mathfrak{qdiff}\circ\kappa)(\textstyle\det_{q}(M)^{-1})=\mathfrak{qdiff}(q^{2\omega_{n}})

We will abuse notation and use
\mathfrak{qdiff}
to also denote its base change to
K
.

Let
W_{\alpha}
be the
K
-vector space spanned by “monomials” of the form

\left\{z_{1}^{k_{1}+\alpha-1}z_{2}^{k_{2}+\alpha-1}\cdots z_{n}^{k_{n}+\alpha-1}\,\middle|\,(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\right\}

We emphasize that the
k_{i}
are now allowed to be negative.

\mathbb{D}_{q}(\mathbf{z}_{n})
naturally acts on this space by

\displaystyle z_{i}\left(z_{1}^{k_{1}+\alpha-1}\cdots z_{n}^{k_{n}+\alpha-1}\right)

\displaystyle=z_{1}^{k_{1}+\alpha-1}\cdots z_{i}^{k_{i}+1+\alpha-1}\cdots z_{n}^{k_{n}+\alpha-1}

\displaystyle T_{q,z_{i}}\left(z_{1}^{k_{1}+\alpha-1}\cdots z_{n}^{k_{n}+\alpha-1}\right)

\displaystyle=q^{k_{i}-1}tz_{1}^{k_{1}+\alpha-1}\cdots z_{n}^{k_{n}+\alpha-1}

Therefore, we obtain an action of
\mathcal{W}^{\circ}\rtimes\mathcal{U}_{t}
on
W_{\alpha}
.
In particular, the
\mathcal{W}^{\circ}
-action is
\mathcal{U}_{t}
-equivariant.
From the formulas (
4.9
) for
\mathfrak{qdiff}
, it is clear that
\mathcal{W}^{\circ}_{0}\rtimes\mathcal{U}
preserves the subspace

W^{0}_{\alpha}:=\mathrm{span}_{K}\left\{z_{1}^{k_{1}+\alpha-1}z_{2}^{k_{2}+\alpha-1}\cdots z_{n}^{k_{n}+\alpha-1}\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\\
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}=0\end{array}\right\}

W^{0}_{\alpha}
will be our replacement for
S_{q}^{n(k-1)}\mathbb{V}
.

Next, we will need a
t
-version of the determinant representation.
Let
\chi^{\pm\alpha}:\mathcal{U}_{t}\rightarrow K
be the characters given by

\begin{gathered}\chi(E_{i})=\chi(F_{i})=0\\
\chi(q^{h})=t^{\pm\langle h,\omega_{n}\rangle}\end{gathered}

We denote by
\mathbb{1}_{-\alpha}\cong K
the dimension 1 representation defined using
\chi^{-\alpha}
.
It is natural to interpret integer shifts of
\alpha
as tensoring by
\mathbb{1}_{k}
.
The zero weight space
W_{\alpha}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{1-\alpha}[0]
is of dimension 1, spanned by
z_{1}^{\alpha-1}\cdots z_{n}^{\alpha-1}\otimes 1
; we thus identify it with
K
.

Finally,
V_{\lambda+(k-1)\delta}
is replaced with the
Verma
module

M_{\lambda}^{\alpha}:=M_{\lambda+(\alpha-1)\delta}

which is simple when
t
is left as a parameter.
With this set, the case of general
t
is in many ways quite similar to that of
t=q^{k}
.

Theorem 5.13

(
[
EK94
]
)
.

We have the following:

(1)

For
\lambda\in P
, there is a nonzero, unique up to constant intertwiner

\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}:M_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\rightarrow M_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\otimes W^{0}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{1-\alpha}

(2)

For
\lambda\in P^{+}
, upon picking consistent normalizations for
\{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\}
, the weighted trace
\widetilde{\varphi}^{\alpha}_{\lambda}
satisfies

P_{\lambda}(q,t)=\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\big/\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}^{\alpha}

We note in passing that
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
is no longer a polynomial but rather a power series.

5.3.2.
Admissible diagrams

Since
M_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
is infinite-dimensional, we can no longer convert the intertwiner
\widetilde{\Phi}^{\alpha}_{\lambda}
into an invariant vector as we we did in
3.5
.
More generally, the graphical calculus covered in
3.3
still applies to
M_{\lambda}
except that it no longer has classical and quantum coevaluations.
This leads to some awkwardness in defining the action of non-invariant elements of
\mathcal{M}
.
Our goal here is to be able to turn elements of
\mathcal{M}
“upside-down”.

We have already made use of diagrammatic calculus for
\mathcal{D}
—let us give a similar construction for
\mathcal{W}^{\circ}
.
The inclusion of the generators
\{\partial_{i}\}
and
\{\xi_{i}\}
come from morphisms
\mathbb{V}^{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}
and
\mathbb{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}
, respectively, and likewise the inclusion of
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(\det_{q}(M))^{-1}
comes from a morphism
\mathbb{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\circ}
.
Since the product
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{W}}
of
\mathcal{W}^{\circ}
is a
\mathcal{U}
-morphism, we can write any element of
\mathcal{W}^{\circ}
as a linear combination of images of morphisms
\mathfrak{d}:X\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\circ}
for some
\mathcal{U}
-module
X
.

Altogether, we have a way to present elements of
\mathcal{M}\cong\mathcal{O}\otimes\kappa(\mathcal{O})\otimes\mathcal{W}^{\circ}
as a linear combination of morphisms of the form

(5.18)

evaluated at various inputs.
We can define a product
*
of two such morphisms by

This yields the product in
\mathcal{M}
upon evaluation.

More generally, the diagram of a morphism
\mathfrak{D}:W_{1}\otimes W_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
is called
admissible
if it is of the form

(5.19)

where the ghost strand only undergoes braidings.
For such a
\mathfrak{D}
, we define
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}:\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\circ}\otimes W_{2}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{U}\otimes W_{1}^{*}
to be the morphism given by

where every morphism inside
\mathfrak{D}
is replaced with its adjoint and the orientation of the ghost strand is reversed.
While
\mathcal{W}^{\circ}
is infinite-dimensional, it is locally finite and thus the coevaluation is done on a finite-dimensional subrepresentation.
Specializing
\mathcal{U}
to act on a finite-dimensional module
U
,
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}
is obtained from
\mathfrak{D}
in terms of partial adjoints.
Thus, this is an operation on morphisms, not just diagrams.

Finally, for a diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{1}
like (
5.18
) and a general admissible diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{2}
, we define the product
\mathfrak{D}_{1}*\mathfrak{D}_{2}
by:

Observe that
\mathfrak{D}_{1}*\mathfrak{D}_{2}
is also admissible.

5.3.3.
Action on linear maps

Let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{tHom}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\hbox{ \tiny{highest weight}}\\
U\hbox{ \tiny{finite-dimensional}}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{U}}\left(M,M\otimes W_{\alpha}\otimes U^{*}\right)\bigg/

\displaystyle\left\langle(f\otimes 1\otimes 1)\circ\psi-\psi\circ f\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}\psi\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{U}}\left(M^{\prime},M\otimes W_{\alpha}\otimes U^{*}\right),\\
f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{U}}(M,M^{\prime})\end{array}\right\rangle

The relations (
5.3.3
) are analogous to the coend relations (
3.14
)—the “t” stands for homomorphisms identified if they have the same trace.
One should view the extra tensorand
U^{*}
as something we will contract away to yield an
K
-linear map
M\rightarrow M\otimes W_{\alpha}
.
Since the Verma module
M_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
is simple, the intertwiners
\{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\}
are linearly independent in
\mathfrak{tHom}_{\alpha}
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{W}}:\mathcal{W}^{\circ}\otimes W_{\alpha}\rightarrow W_{\alpha}
be the action map.
For an admissible diagram
\mathfrak{D}:W_{1}\otimes W_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
as in
(\ref{Admiss})
and

\phi\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{U}}\left(M,M\otimes W_{\alpha}\otimes U^{*}\right)\subset\mathfrak{tHom}_{\alpha}

we define

\mathfrak{D}\star\phi:V\otimes M\rightarrow(V\otimes M)\otimes W_{\alpha}\otimes(W_{1}\otimes W_{2}\otimes U)^{*}

to be the class of the morphism:

(5.22)

Such a morphism is well-defined for infinite-dimensional
M
because
M
only undergoes braidings.
Moreover, any
f
as in the relations (
5.3.3
) can pass through such braidings.
This implies that
\mathfrak{D}\star\phi
is independent of the representative of the class of
\phi
.
We leave it as a drawing exercise to see that for a diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{1}
of the form (
5.18
) and an admissible diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{2}
, we have

(5.23)

\mathfrak{D}_{1}\star\left(\mathfrak{D}_{2}\star\phi\right)=\left(\mathfrak{D}_{1}*\mathfrak{D}_{2}\right)\star\phi

Consider now the space of
K
-linear maps:

\displaystyle\mathrm{tHom}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\bigoplus_{M\hbox{ \tiny{is highest weight}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{R_{q}}\left(M,M\otimes W_{\alpha}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{1-\alpha}\right)\bigg/

\displaystyle\left\langle(f\otimes 1)\circ\psi-\psi\circ f\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}\psi\in\mathrm{Hom}_{R_{q}}\left(M^{\prime},M\otimes W_{\alpha}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{1-\alpha}\right),\\
f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{U}}(M,M^{\prime})\end{array}\right\rangle

For the class of
\phi:M\rightarrow M\otimes W_{\alpha}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{1-\alpha}
in
\mathrm{tHom}_{\alpha}
, we define an operation by each of the three tensor components of
\mathcal{M}\cong\mathcal{O}\otimes\partial_{\triangleright}(\mathcal{O})\otimes\mathcal{W}^{\circ}
:

•

for
v^{*}\otimes v\in V^{*}\otimes V\subset\mathcal{O}\otimes 1\subset\mathcal{D}
, define

(5.26)

\begin{gathered}(v^{*}\otimes v)\star\phi:V\otimes M\rightarrow V\otimes M\otimes W_{\alpha}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{1-\alpha}\\
\big((v^{*}\otimes v)\star\phi\big)(x\otimes m)=v^{*}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-11.38931pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.92825pt}}_{{\kern-6.33623pt{s}\kern 3.92825pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}r^{{\kern-11.38931pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.92825pt}}_{{\kern-6.33623pt{s}\kern 1.67627pt}}}x)\big[S(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76944pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.26389pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{t}}}}r^{{\kern-9.70157pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.02084pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76944pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.26389pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.67627pt}}_{{\kern-3.42801pt{s}\kern 1.67627pt}}})\bullet w)\otimes 1\right)\blacktriangleright\phi\left(\mathchoice{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}{r^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.77432pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29686pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64063pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{s}}}}m\right)\end{gathered}

where the
\blacktriangleright
means acting on the output of
\phi
via the
\mathcal{U}
- and
\mathcal{W}^{\circ}
-actions.

Because the input of
\phi
and
M
-tensorand of the output of
\phi
is only acted on by elements of
\mathcal{U}
, these operations are well-defined on the quotient (
5.3.3
).

A priori
, we do not know that they piece together to form an action of
\mathcal{M}
.
To that end, let:

•

\mathrm{tHom}_{\alpha}^{D}
be the subspace of
\mathrm{tHom}_{\alpha}
generated by these operations from the intertwiners
\{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\}_{\lambda\in P}
(the
D
is for “diagram”);

•

\mathrm{Int}_{\alpha}\subset\mathrm{tHom}_{\alpha}^{D}
be the span of
\{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\}_{\lambda\in P}
;

•

\mathrm{Int}_{\alpha}^{+}\subset\mathrm{Int}_{\alpha}
be the span of
\{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\}_{\lambda\in P^{+}}
.

Finally, we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{[t]}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{C}(q)[t^{\pm 1}]\otimes\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{K}

\displaystyle:=K\otimes\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{C}(q,t)\otimes\mathcal{M}

Lemma 5.14
.

The operations (
5.26
)-(
5.28
) define actions of
\mathcal{M}_{[t]}
and
\mathcal{M}_{K}
on
\mathrm{tHom}_{\alpha}^{D}
.
Under this action,
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{U}}
preserves
\mathrm{Int}_{\alpha}
.

Remark 5.15
.

The formulas (
5.26
)-(
5.28
) should define an action of
\mathcal{M}
on the entire space
\mathrm{tHom}_{\alpha}
.
We leave the proof to a more skillful scholar of the Yang-Baxter equation.

Proof.

On
\mathrm{tHom}_{\alpha}^{D}
, the operations (
5.26
)-(
5.28
) are obtained by contracting the diagram actions on
\mathfrak{tHom}_{\alpha}
with
\phi
set equal to an intertwiner
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
.
Equation (
5.23
) implies that the diagram actions yield an
\mathcal{M}
-action upon contraction.
From the diagram action (
5.22
) with
W_{1}=W_{2}=\mathbb{1}
, it is evident that
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{U}}
sends
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
to some other intertwiner.
As pointed out in the proof of Lemma
5.7
,
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{U}}\subset\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{W}^{\circ}_{0}
, and thus acting with an element of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{U}}
sends
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
to some intertwiner

V\otimes M_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\rightarrow V\otimes M_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\otimes W_{\alpha}^{0}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{1-\alpha}

for some finite-dimensional
V
.
Using the modified coend relation (
5.3.3
), this can be rewritten as a linear combination of
\{\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\}_{\lambda\in P}
.
∎

5.3.4.
Radial parts at generic parameters

Let us now discuss quantum Hamiltonian reduction.
Consider the left ideal
\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}\subset\mathcal{M}_{[t]}
given by

\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}:=\mathcal{M}_{[t]}\left(\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(M)-q^{2}t^{-2}I\right)\subset\mathcal{M}_{[t]}

Lemma 5.16
.

The
\mathcal{M}_{[t]}^{\mathcal{U}}
action on
\mathrm{Int}_{\alpha}
factors through the subspace
\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{U}}
.

Proof.

We approach this similarly to how we proved Proposition
4.16
.
Namely, we prove that the action map

\mathcal{M}_{[t]}\otimes\mathrm{Int}_{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathrm{tHom}_{\alpha}^{D}

factors through
\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
.
To that end, we compute the action of
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(M)
on
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
.
Using
(\ref{DMomentDia})
, we get:

For the first equality, we applied the modified coend relation (
5.3.3
).
From (
3.6
), one can see that the final diagram is equal to the action of the diagram for
q^{2}t^{-2}\mathrm{ev}_{\mathbb{V}}
, which yields the entries of
q^{2}t^{-2}I
upon contraction.
∎

We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}^{[t]}

\displaystyle:=\left(\mathcal{M}_{[t]}\big/\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}\right)^{\mathcal{U}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=K\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}

Like in Proposition
4.15
,
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}^{[t]}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
are in fact algebras.
Let us also define

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}^{[t]}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{C}(q)[t^{pm1}]\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\mathrm{IV}}^{[t]}

\displaystyle:=\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}^{[t]}\bigg/\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{IV}}^{[t]}\cap\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}\right)\right)^{\mathcal{U}}

Proposition 5.17
.

\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
is generated by

\left\{\mathrm{qcoev}_{\wedge^{r}_{q}\mathbb{V}}(1),\partial_{\triangleright}\mathrm{qcoev}_{\wedge_{q}^{r}\mathbb{V}^{*}}(1)\right\}_{r=1}^{n}\cup\left\{\textstyle\det_{q}(A)^{-1},\textstyle\det_{q}(B)\right\}

Proof.

Because tensoring is right exact, there is a surjective map

\mathcal{A}_{k,\mathrm{IV}}\rightarrow\left[\mathbb{C}(q)[t^{\pm 1}]\bigg/(t-q^{k})\right]\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\mathrm{IV}}^{[t]}

For
k>n
, the analogous generation statement for
\mathcal{A}_{k}
is true due to Theorem
5.12
and Lemma
2.5
.
The result follows from applying Nakayam’s Lemma to each (finitely generated) bigraded piece and then localizing.
∎

Lemma 5.18
.

The actions of
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}^{[t]}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
on
\mathrm{Int}_{\alpha}
preserve the subspace
\mathrm{Int}_{\alpha}^{+}
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the statement for
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
.
We then consider each of the generators given in Proposition
5.17
.
The elements
\partial_{\triangleright}\mathrm{qcoev}_{\wedge_{q}^{r}\mathbb{V}^{*}}(1)
and
\det_{q}(B)
act by inserting central elements of
\mathcal{U}
into
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
, and thus they act diagonally.
For
\mathrm{qcoev}_{\wedge^{r}_{q}\mathbb{V}}(1)
and
\det_{q}(A)^{-1}
, note that the action of either on
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
yields the intertwiner

\mathrm{id}_{V}\otimes\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}:V\otimes M_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\rightarrow V\otimes M_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\otimes W_{\alpha}^{0}

for some finite-dimensional
\mathcal{U}
-module
V
.
By Lemma 5 of
[
BGG71
]
,
V\otimes M_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
decomposes into a direct sum of
\{M_{\mu}^{\alpha}\}
for finitely many
\mu\in P
, and thus

\mathrm{id}_{V}\otimes\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}=\sum_{\mu}c_{\lambda\mu}(q,t)\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}

for some
\{c_{\lambda\mu}(q,t)\}\subset K
.
We will show that we can set
c_{\lambda\mu}(q,t)=0
for some
\mu\not\in P^{+}
and for
\mu\in P^{+}
,
c_{\lambda\mu}(q,t)
is a Pieri coefficient
[
Mac15
, VI.6]
.

To do so, let us review some details from the proof of Theorem 2 of
[
EK94
]
.
Let
U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})\subset\mathcal{U}
be the subalgebra generated by the
\{F_{i}\}
and let
\{\beta_{\}}
be a weight basis of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}_{-})
.
Picking a highest weight vector
v_{\nu}^{\alpha}
for
M_{\nu}^{\alpha}
, we obtain a weight basis
\{\beta_{a}v_{\nu}^{\alpha}\}
for
M_{\nu}^{\alpha}
.
We will also make use of the natural monomial basis
\{m_{c}\}
of
W_{\alpha}^{0}
, which is also a weight basis.
An intertwiner is determined by the coefficients
\{{}_{\nu}\widetilde{R}_{bc}^{a}(q,t)\}\subset K
such that:

\widetilde{\Phi}_{\nu}^{\alpha}(\beta_{a}v_{\nu}^{\alpha})=\sum_{b,c}{}_{\nu}\widetilde{R}_{bc}^{a}(q,t)\beta_{b}v_{\nu}^{\alpha}\otimes m_{c}

Given
k>0
and
\nu\in P^{+}
, the subset of
\{\beta_{a}\}
such that

\nu-\mathrm{wt}(\beta_{a})=\sum_{i}n_{i}\epsilon_{i}

with
0\leq n_{i}\leq k
likewise provides a basis for the finite-dimensional module
V_{\nu}^{k}
.
Likewise, an appropriate subset of the monomials
\{m_{c}\}
give a basis of
U_{k}
.
The intertwiner
\Phi_{\nu}^{k}
is determined by the coefficients
\{{}_{\nu}^{k}R_{bc}^{a}(q)\}\subset\mathbb{C}(q)
:

\Phi_{\nu}^{k}(\beta_{a}v_{\nu}^{k})=\sum_{b,c}{}_{\nu}^{k}R_{bc}^{a}(q)\beta_{b}v_{\nu}^{k}\otimes m_{c}

In
loc. cit.
, the authors showed that given
(\nu,a,b,c)
, we have

{}_{\nu}\widetilde{R}_{bc}^{a}(q,q^{k})={}_{\nu}^{k}R_{bc}^{a}(q)

for all
k
sufficiently large for the right-hand-side to make sense.

Now, let
v\in V
be a weight vector and consider
v\otimes\beta_{a_{\lambda}}v_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
.
We can write this tensor as

v\otimes\beta_{a_{\lambda}}v_{\lambda}^{\alpha}=\sum_{\mu}\sum_{\ell}d_{a_{\mu}^{\ell}}(q,t)\beta_{a_{\mu}^{\ell}}v_{\mu}^{\alpha}

for some coefficients
\{d_{a_{\mu}^{\ell}}(q,t)\}\subset K
.
Consider any
k
large enough that:

•

{}_{\lambda}\widetilde{R}_{bc}^{a_{\lambda}}(q,q^{k})={}_{\lambda}^{k}R_{bc}^{a_{\lambda}}(q)
for the
bc
-indices appearing in
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}(\beta_{a_{\lambda}}v_{\lambda}^{\alpha})
;

•

for
\mu\in P^{+}
,
{}_{\mu}\widetilde{R}_{bc}^{a_{\mu}^{\ell}}(q,q^{k})={}_{\mu}^{k}R_{bc}^{a_{\mu}^{\ell}}(q)
for the
bc
-indices appearing in the evaluations
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\beta_{a_{\mu}^{\ell}}v_{\mu}^{\alpha})
;

•

d_{a_{\mu}^{\ell}}(q,q^{k})
is well-defined for all
a_{\mu}^{\ell}
.

The Pieri rules for
P_{\lambda}(q,q^{k})
yield the equality

v\otimes\widetilde{\Phi}_{\lambda}^{\alpha}(\beta_{a_{\lambda}}v_{\lambda}^{\alpha})\bigg|_{t\mapsto q^{k}}=\sum_{\mu}c_{\lambda\mu}(q,t)\sum_{a_{\mu}^{\ell}}d_{a_{\mu}^{\ell}}(q,t)\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\beta_{a_{\mu}^{\ell}}v_{\mu}^{\alpha})\bigg|_{t\mapsto q^{k}}

Here, by
t\mapsto q^{k}
, we mean specialize the coefficients of the output basis
\{\beta_{b}v_{\mu}^{\alpha}\otimes m_{c}\}
.
Since this equality holds at
t=q^{k}
for infinitely many values of
k
, it also holds for general
t
.
∎

The action on
\mathrm{Int}_{\alpha}^{+}
yields an algebra homomorphism

\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha}:\mathcal{A}^{[t]}_{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}_{K}(\Lambda^{\pm}_{n}(q,t))

that we also call the
radial parts
map.
Through analysis similar to what was done in
3.5.3
, the analogue of Proposition
4.18
and its proof holds in this case as well:

Proposition 5.19
.

Upon base change to
K
, the image of
\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha}
contains the image of
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
under
\mathfrak{r}
:

\mathfrak{rad}_{k}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}\right)\supset\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)\right)

This inclusion respects the bigradings and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha}\left(\mathrm{qcoev}_{\wedge_{q}^{r}\mathbb{V}}(1)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathbf{s}e_{r}(\mathbf{X}_{n})\mathbf{s}\right)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha}\left(\partial_{\triangleright}\mathrm{qcoev}_{\wedge_{q}^{r}\mathbb{V}^{*}}(1)\right)

\displaystyle=q^{r(n-1)}\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathbf{s}e_{r}(\mathbf{Y}^{-1}_{n})\mathbf{s}\right)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha}\left(\textstyle\det_{q}(A)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathbf{s}\mathbf{X}^{-1}_{n}\mathbf{s}\right)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha}\left(\textstyle\det_{q}(B)\right)

\displaystyle=q^{-n(n-1)}\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathbf{s}\mathbf{Y}_{n}\mathbf{s}\right)

Theorem 5.20
.

Upon base change to
K
, the radial parts map is an isomorphism onto
\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)\right)
.

Proof.

On the generators from Proposition
5.17
, one can see that specializing
\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha}
to
t=q^{k}
yields
\mathfrak{rad}_{k}
, and thus this is true for the entirety of
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\mathrm{IV}}
.
Therefore, a torsion-free element of a bigraded piece
\mathcal{A}^{[t]}_{\alpha,\mathrm{IV}}[a,b]
in the kernel of
\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha}
must vanish at infinitely many specializations
t=q^{k}
.
Since
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\mathrm{IV}}^{[t]}[a,b]
is finitely generated and
\mathbb{C}(q)[t^{\pm 1}]
is a PID, this implies that the kernel is torsion and hence disappears upon base change to
K
.
∎

Appendix A
Modular transformations

Here, we investigate the relationship between the quantum radial parts map and the
SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-action on the DAHA.
The main result is that our isomorphism sends a
slope subalgebra
of
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
to a subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
generated by quantum traces of monodromy matrices over a suitable cycle on the torus.

A.1.
SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-action on DAHA

W begin by reviewing the
SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-action on the DAHA, as presented in 3.7 of
[
Che05
]
.
Recall that
SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
has a presentation with generators

\sigma=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\,\tau=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}

and relations

\sigma^{4}=1,\,(\sigma\tau)^{3}=\sigma^{2}

It acts on
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
by
R
-algebra automorphisms given by

\displaystyle\sigma(T_{i})

\displaystyle=T_{i},

\displaystyle\sigma(X_{i})

\displaystyle=Y_{i}^{-1},

\displaystyle\sigma(Y_{i})

\displaystyle=T_{w_{0}}^{-1}X_{n-i+1}T_{w_{0}},

\displaystyle\tau(T_{i})

\displaystyle=T_{i},

\displaystyle\tau(X_{1}\cdots X_{i})

\displaystyle=q^{i}(Y_{1}\cdots Y_{i})(X_{1}\cdots X_{i}),

\displaystyle\tau(Y_{i})

\displaystyle=Y_{i}

where
w_{0}\in\Sigma_{n}
is the longest element.
Since the generators fix
\{T_{i}\}
, it follows that
SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
acts on
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
.

It will be easier to work with a generating set smaller than the one we used previously in
2.2.2
:

Lemma A.1

(
[
FFJ+11
]
Lemma 5.2)
.

\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
is generated by the four elements:

\left\{\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{X}_{n})\mathbf{s},\,\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{X}_{n}^{-1})\mathbf{s},\,\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{Y}_{n})\mathbf{s},\,\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{Y}_{n}^{-1})\mathbf{s}\right\}

Proposition A.2
.

We have:

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{X}_{n})\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{Y}_{n}^{-1})\mathbf{s},

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{Y}_{n})\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{X}_{n})\mathbf{s},

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{X}_{n}^{-1})\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{Y}_{n})\mathbf{s},

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{Y}_{n}^{-1})\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{s}e_{1}(\mathbf{X}_{n}^{-1})\mathbf{s}

A.2.
Gaussian

The following element can be defined in a suitable completion of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
:

\gamma:=\frac{24t^{n}\log(t)^{2}}{n(n^{2}-1)\log(q)}\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\log(Y_{i})^{2}}{\log(q)}\right)

Recall from
2.3
the polynomial representation
\mathfrak{r}
of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
, which is faithful.
We can view
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
via its image
\mathfrak{r}(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t))\subset\mathrm{End}(\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(q,t))
.
Thus, rather than go into detail about the completion, we will just confirm that
\mathfrak{r}(\gamma)
is a well-defined operator.

Proposition A.3

(
[
DFK19
]
)
.

The following hold:

(1)

The action of
\gamma
on
\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(q,t)
is well-defined:

(A.1)

\mathfrak{r}(\gamma)P_{\lambda}(q,t)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}q^{\lambda_{i}^{2}}t^{(n-2i)\lambda_{i}}P_{\lambda}(q,t)

(2)

The action of
\tau\in SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
on
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)
is equal to
\mathrm{ad}_{\gamma}
.

A.3.
Fourier transform

Lemma
5.7
can also be adapted to the case of generic
t
.
It implies that we can define an algebra automorphism on
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
by defining it on
\mathcal{D}
provided that it preserves invariants as well as the image of the moment map.
The analogue of the automorphism
\sigma
is given by Definition-Proposition 6.11 of
[
Jor14
]
:

Proposition A.4
.

The following defines an algebra automorphism
\mathfrak{F}
of
\mathcal{D}
:

\displaystyle(1\otimes\mathfrak{F})(A^{\pm 1})

\displaystyle=B^{\pm 1},

\displaystyle(1\otimes\mathfrak{F})(B^{\pm 1})

\displaystyle=q^{\mp 2n}BA^{\mp 1}B^{-1}

Observe that the moment map
\mu_{\mathcal{D}}
(
4.11
) is left unchanged by
\mathfrak{F}
.
It is also clear that
\mathfrak{F}
sends quantum trace elements to other quantum trace elements.
A corollary of Proposition
5.4
is that
\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{U}}
is generated by quantum trace elements, so this implies that
\mathfrak{F}
preserves
\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{U}}
.
Therefore,
\mathfrak{F}
descends to an automorphism of
\mathcal{A}_{k}
.

Lemma A.5
.

We have the following equalities in
\mathcal{D}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}\left(\mathrm{tr}_{q}(A^{\pm 1})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}_{q}(B^{\pm 1}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}\left(\mathrm{tr}_{q}(B^{\pm 1})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}_{q}(A^{\mp 1})

Proof.

Only the identities in the second column are nontrivial.
Notice we have:

The constant
q^{2n}
comes from using Proposition
3.3
to compute
\nu^{2}
on
\mathbb{V}=V_{\omega_{1}}
.
For
\mathrm{tr}_{q}(BAB^{-1})=q^{-2n}\mathrm{tr}_{q}(A)
, the calculations are similar.
∎

Corollary A.6
.

Under the isomorphism
\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha}
,
\mathfrak{F}
induces the automorphism
\sigma
on
\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,q^{k})\right)
.

A.4.
B
-Dehn twist

Here, we will view the ribbon element
\nu
as
1\otimes\nu\otimes 1\in\mathcal{O}\rtimes\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{2}
.
In this manner, we can make sense of
\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha}(\nu)
by having it act on intertwiners, whereby it acts by insertion into the input.
Combining Proposition
3.3
and Theorem
3.11
gives us:

(A.2)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha}(\nu)P_{\lambda}(q,t)

\displaystyle=q^{-\langle\lambda,\lambda\rangle-\alpha\langle\lambda,2\rho\rangle-(\alpha-1)(\alpha+1)\langle\rho,\rho\rangle}P_{\lambda}(q,t)

\displaystyle=q^{-(\alpha-1)(\alpha+1)\langle\rho,\rho\rangle}\prod_{i=1}^{n}q^{-\lambda_{i}^{2}}t^{-\lambda_{i}(n-2i)}P_{\lambda}(q,t)

Comparing the
\lambda
-dependent parts of the eigenvalue with (
A.1
) gives us:

Proposition A.7
.

We have

q^{-(\alpha-1)(\alpha+1)\langle\rho,\rho\rangle}\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha}(\nu^{-1})=\mathfrak{r}(\gamma)

From (
A.2
), it is clear that conjugation by
\nu
preserves the
\ker(\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha})
and thus defines an algebra automorphism of
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
.
Moreover, by Propositions
A.3
and
A.7
, this action coincides with
\tau^{-1}
on
\mathfrak{r}(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t))
.
An analogue of the following lemma was proved in
[
Fai19
]
in the setting of finite-dimensional Hopf algebras, although we note that our proof is quite different:

Lemma A.8
.

Conjugation by
\nu
yields the algebra automorphism on
\mathcal{D}
induced by:

\displaystyle(1\otimes\mathrm{ad}_{\nu})(A)

\displaystyle=q^{n}B^{-1}A,

\displaystyle(1\otimes\mathrm{ad}_{\nu})(B)

\displaystyle=B

Proof.

Only the first equation is nontrivial.
We use (
3.13
) to commute
\nu
past an
A
-matrix element:

On the other hand, we have:

The
q^{n}
compensates for the extra loop.
∎

A.5.
Slope versus cycles

Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)_{0}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\mathbf{s}e_{r}(\mathbf{X}_{n})\mathbf{s},\,\mathbf{s}e_{r}(\mathbf{X}_{n}^{-1})\mathbf{s}\,\middle|\,1\leq r\leq n\right\rangle

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)_{\infty}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\mathbf{s}e_{r}(\mathbf{Y}_{n})\mathbf{s},\,\mathbf{s}e_{r}(\mathbf{Y}_{n}^{-1})\mathbf{s}\,\middle|\,1\leq r\leq n\right\rangle

For
\frac{b}{a}\in\mathbb{Q}
with
a
and
b
relatively prime, define

\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)_{\frac{b}{a}}:=g\left(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)_{0}\right)

for any
g\in SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
g(1,0)=(a,b)
.
Such a
g
can always be constructed using the Euclidean algorithm.
On the other hand, the definition does not depend on
g
since the stabilizer of
(1,0)
is generated by

\eta:=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}=\sigma\tau\sigma^{-1}

and
\eta
acts trivially on
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)_{0}
.
We call
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)_{\frac{b}{a}}
the
slope
\frac{b}{a}
subalgebra
.

Analogously, we consider the following subalgebras of
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}^{(1,0)}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\mathrm{tr}_{q}(A^{m})\,\middle|\,m\in\mathbb{Z}\right\rangle\cong\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}^{(0,1)}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle\mathrm{tr}_{q}(B^{m})\,\middle|\,m\in\mathbb{Z}\right\rangle\cong\partial_{\triangleright}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{U}})

If we view
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
as a quantized character variety for the torus (cf.
[
AS96
,
BZBJ18a
,
BZBJ18b
]
), then these subalgebras are generated by quantized traces of monodromy matrices over the
a
- and
b
-cycles, respectively.
Our analysis in
3.5
shows that:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}^{(1,0)}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}^{(0,1)}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)_{\infty}\right)

For
\frac{b}{a}\in\mathbb{Q}
with
a
and
b
relatively prime, we define

\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}^{(a,b)}:=\mathfrak{rad}_{\alpha}^{-1}\left(\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(q,t)_{\frac{b}{a}}\right)\right)

By Corollary
A.6
and Proposition
A.7
, we can obtain
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}^{(a,b)}
by applying the appropriate combinations of
\mathfrak{F}
and
\mathrm{ad}_{\nu}
to
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}^{(1,0)}
.
Since
\sigma
and
\tau
generate
SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, we can make sense of the action of any
g\in SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
on
\mathcal{A}_{k}
in this manner.

We end with an observation relating
\frac{b}{a}
and the
(a,b)
-cycle on the torus.
For a product
\Pi
of
A
- and
B
-matrices, we define its
support

\mathrm{supp}(\Pi):=(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}

where:

•

a
is the sum of all exponents of
A
-matrices appearing in
\Pi
;

•

b
is the sum of all exponents of
B
-matrices appearing in
\Pi
.

Thus, if
\Pi
can be viewed as a monodromy over the
(a,b)
-cycle.
The following is an easy consequence of the definition of
\mathfrak{F}
and Lemma
A.8
:

Corollary A.9
.

For
g\in SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, we have

g\left(\mathrm{tr}_{q}(A^{m})\right)=c\mathrm{tr}_{q}(\Pi^{m})

for some
c\in K
and product
\Pi
of
A
- and
B
-matrices such that
\mathrm{supp}(\Pi)=g(1,0)
.

Thus,
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}^{(a,b)}
is generated by certain quantum traces of monodromy matrices supported on the
(a,b)
-cycle.
